The Signature Trade Databases Database

Exhaustive

million in progress

march to a million: in progress

Archive records: 24 / 1,000,000 target · 24 records (populated from the live archive index)

This pair catalogs the notable public trade databases of the world: the trade-flow repositories, tariff databases, and trade models that record what countries buy and sell. Each record gives the history of one database, its public record — what public information exists, where it lives, its license, and how to reach it — and what the Signature edition adds. Every record is a catalog entry about the database, never a rebuild of the live database itself, and every record cross-links to the live database where one exists. The Signature edition carries original Signature-made designs for the same twenty-four subjects: how the Signature edition would organize, present, and teach each database. Each Signature record is a Signature design study, labeled as a design, never presented as an established public record. Distinction, stated plainly: the live network already holds hundreds of single-trade databases, but no catalog of the trade databases themselves — no record of UN Comtrade, the WTO statistics service, WITS, the tariff databases, the trade models, or the shipment indexes as databases. This pair is that catalog: one full record per database, ordered simple to compounded to complex. Endpoints and licenses are stated only where published; where no public endpoint or license is published, the record says so plainly, and no access data is invented. Twenty-four full records, ordered simple to compounded to complex, each written in human-readable sentences and paragraphs, each carrying a full history, the public record in full, a Signature version, a dataset note, a science proof, a math proof of the spot-check statistics, a formal statement, a scientific equation, a proof sketch, a runnable demo program whose output is reproduced on the page, and a named specialist AI. Every demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample, runs the sample twice, and matches the two SHA-256 hashes; no demo queries the live database, and no record presents a design as an established public record.

Specialist AI: JAH Signature Trade Designer

JAH Signature Trade Designer answers questions about this database, explains any record in plain sentences, compares records side by side on the same fields, and helps a reader follow the ladder from simple to compounded to complex. It never presents a design as an established public record.

Boundless generator

The generator is seeded and deterministic, throttled to at most 100 records per call, duplicate-checked by deterministic hash ids, and hard-stopped at 1,000,000,000 records. Every generated record is labeled src "generated". Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files. Open the page script to use window.TradeDatabasesSignatureTools.

Validation engine

Each record is checked for a complete title, its own record kind, its own edition slug and id prefix, a full prose description, a subject, a problem of at least 400 characters, a method, a history, a public record, a Signature version, a dataset note, limits and cautions, key figures, an evidence label with its reason, a verification path, a science proof, a math proof, a formal statement, a scientific equation, a proof sketch, a named source, a JCC v1 condensed code with a recomputed key, and a demo whose stored output byte-matches a fresh run.

Records

UN Comtrade Rank 1 · simple

Evidence: design-study Design study: this record describes an original Signature design for UN Comtrade, a design study that presents no public fact as established and no design as a record.

UN Comtrade is the official repository of international merchandise trade statistics, maintained by the United Nations Statistics Division. It holds the trade flows that member states report, organized by commodity and partner, and it is the reference source behind most published world trade figures.

The question behind UN Comtrade is a catalog question: what does this trade database hold, who keeps it, and how does a reader reach the public record inside it? UN Comtrade is the official repository of international merchandise trade statistics, maintained by the United Nations Statistics Division. It holds the trade flows that member states report, organized by commodity and partner, and it is the reference source behind most published world trade figures. That is the whole of the question, and this record answers it in order: what the database is, where its public record lives, what license and access method govern it, and what the Signature edition designs beside it. The question matters because trade databases are the shelves of the public record: a reader who cannot find the shelf cannot check the fact, and a fact that cannot be checked is only a rumor with good manners. Cataloguing the catalogues is therefore ordinary library work, done one database at a time, with the access terms stated plainly and nothing invented where the publisher has published nothing. A reader who opens UN Comtrade is looking for world merchandise trade: the entries, the records, and the classifications that make up the public record of world merchandise trade. The database answers by holding those records in one place, in one classification, with one search, instead of leaving them scattered across a hundred publisher sites and a thousand documents. That is the service a trade database performs: it gathers, it classifies, it keeps the record searchable, and it lets a reader ask a question and get an answer that can be cited. The public record inside it is described in this entry in full: what information exists, where it lives, what license covers it, and how a reader reaches it, whether through a website, a download, or a published endpoint. Where no public endpoint is published, this record says so plainly, because an honest catalog never invents a door that is not there. The Signature version of UN Comtrade is a separate matter, and it is kept separate in this record. The public edition describes what exists; the Signature edition designs what could be built beside it: an original Signature design that teaches, presents, or re-organizes the same subject without copying the public database and without presenting a design as an established record. The two editions share a title and a subject, and they differ in exactly that way: one reports, the other designs. This record therefore serves two readers at once: the reader who wants the public facts about UN Comtrade, and the reader who wants to see what a Signature edition of the same subject would look like. Both readers get the same honesty: the public facts are stated as published, the design is labelled a design, and the ceiling line at the end of every record states the generator's limit in plain words.

History

The database descends from the United Nations commodity trade statistics first compiled in the 1960s, and the modern Comtrade service, Comtrade Plus, is the current public front door. In 2016 the platform was rebuilt as Comtrade Plus with a public API. The Harmonized System revisions HS 2017 and HS 2022 were absorbed as new classification editions, and the service keeps adding monthly reporter data as member states file it. The history above is the published history: dates and owners as the database's own publications state them, with later developments carried in the public-record section below. A reader who wants the full chronology should read the database's own about page and its published documentation, which are named in the verification path. This record gives the shape of the story, not a day-by-day chronicle, and it says so where a date is approximate rather than exact. What matters for the catalog is not the day the database opened but the continuity: the record has been kept, the classification has been maintained, and the public can still reach it, which is why it belongs in this pair. The dates in this record are the dates the operator publishes; where the operator gives only a decade or an era, this record gives the decade or the era rather than inventing a year.

Public record

UN Comtrade is the official repository of international merchandise trade statistics, maintained by the United Nations Statistics Division. It holds the trade flows that member states report, organized by commodity and partner, and it is the reference source behind most published world trade figures. It covers more than 200 reporting economies and trade reported since 1962, with annual and monthly flows in the Harmonized System and SITC classifications. Access is free through the Comtrade Plus web service, which offers a public API (version 1) with a free tier and bulk downloads; the service asks users to cite the United Nations as the source. That is the public record in outline. In detail, the record holds the subject in the form the operator publishes it: entries, classifications, and search tools, with the operator's own documentation as the guide to what each field means. The license and access terms above are the whole of the terms this record states, because they are the whole of the terms the publisher has published: where a term is unpublished, this record says so rather than guessing. A reader who needs more than the outline should follow the verification path, which names the database's own site and documentation; the catalog entry is a map to the record, and the record itself lives where the operator keeps it. The public record is also a living record: the operator adds entries, corrects entries, and revises classifications, so the counts in this entry are the counts as published at the time of writing, and a reader should check the live database for the current figure. Nothing in this record is a copy of the database itself: the catalog entry describes the database, and the database itself is reached through the links and the verification path.

Methods

Open the database's own site from the verification path and read its about page to confirm the operator and the coverage; Search the database for one world merchandise trade entry you already know, and check that the entry you find matches what you expected; Read the license and access terms on the operator's own terms page, and note whether a public endpoint is published; View or download one sample of the data in the form the operator publishes it, and check its classification against this record's description; Run the demo program beside this record and compare its output with the stored output, to confirm the sampling statistics; Read the Signature design in the signature edition and note what it adds without copying the public database.

Signature version

The Signature edition designs a Comtrade companion: a Signature trade atlas that re-presents the same flows as a plain-language atlas, with each flow shown as a route between two places, a one-paragraph story of what the flow is, and a worked example of reading a single Harmonized System code. The design adds a classroom layer that teaches the Harmonized System from the goods up, and a comparison view that puts two countries' trade side by side. The design is original Signature work: it borrows the subject of the public database and nothing else, and it is labelled a design study in every place it appears, so that no reader mistakes it for an established public record. The design is judged as a design: whether it teaches the subject plainly, whether it presents the subject honestly, and whether a reader who follows it learns the subject rather than a copy of the database. The Signature edition keeps the same title and the same ladder rank as the public edition, so the two can be read side by side, and the difference between them is exactly the difference between reporting what exists and designing what could be built beside it.

Limits and cautions

The limits of this record are the limits of a catalog entry. It describes UN Comtrade; it does not contain it, and it cannot answer a question that only the database itself can answer, such as the current value of a single entry or the current text of a single record. The counts and dates in this record are snapshots from the operator's publications at the time of writing, and a living database will have moved on; the record says where the live figure can be checked, and a reader should check it. Where the operator publishes no public endpoint or no public license, this record says so, and the absence is a fact about the operator's publishing, not a gap in the catalog. The Signature design beside this record is a design study, not an established public record, and it must not be cited as evidence about the real database. Finally, this record is one of twenty-four in its pair, and it is written to be read beside the other twenty-three: no single entry carries the whole subject, and the field guide index record exists to hold the map.

Key figures

  • The member states' national statistical and customs offices (the reporters whose filings make up the data)
  • The Harmonized System Committee of the World Customs Organization (keeper of the classification)

Dataset

It covers more than 200 reporting economies and trade reported since 1962, with annual and monthly flows in the Harmonized System and SITC classifications. The dataset behind this record is the published record of the database itself: the counts, the classifications, and the access terms stated above, taken from the operator's own publications and from the live database's own pages. It is not a dump of the database's contents, which would be a rebuild rather than a catalog entry, and this pair never rebuilds the databases it catalogues. The dataset is therefore small and honest: one entry per database, with the facts that let a reader find the database, understand what it holds, and reach it. The demo beside this record samples from a fixed seed to show the statistics of sampling; it does not reproduce the database's contents, and it says so in its own output. Where the operator publishes bulk downloads, this record notes them in the public-record section; where it does not, the record says so. The figures in this entry are the figures the operator publishes, and any figure that is an estimate is labelled as an estimate in the text. The counts in this entry are the counts as published at the time of writing, and a living database grows, so a reader should check the live database for the current figure.

Science proof

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. The demo runs that audit twice with the same seed and hashes both results, so a fresh run verifies the arithmetic exactly. The audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic; it does not query the live database and it proves nothing about the live holdings, which must be checked against the published source itself. The science of Comtrade is the science of reconciling reports: every flow is reported twice, once as an export and once as an import, and the two reports rarely agree exactly, because valuation, timing, and classification differ. In plain words, the demo draws 25 entries at random with a fixed seed across 4 categories, so each category should receive about 6.25 entries by chance alone. The demo's simulated run gave counts {"exports": 4, "imports": 9, "re-exports": 3, "transit": 9} and a chi-square of 4.920. A chi-square near zero is exactly what a flat random draw produces; a large chi-square would need a reason, and the audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic, not that the live database is balanced.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic: expected per category E = 25 / 4 = 6.25. The demo's simulated counts were {"exports": 4, "imports": 9, "re-exports": 3, "transit": 9}. Chi-square = the sum over the 4 categories of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected, which comes to 4.920. The demo runs the sample twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

χ² = Σ (O_i − E)² / E, E = trials/categories (stated, not machine-checked)

Scientific equation

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math
import random

title = 'UN Comtrade'
entries = 200
categories = ['exports', 'imports', 're-exports', 'transit']
trials = 25
seed = 1000
claimed_note = 'As published: official merchandise trade statistics reported by more than 200 economies, from 1962 to the present, free to use with citation.'

def run_once():
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    counts = {c: 0 for c in categories}
    for _ in range(trials):
        counts[rng.choice(categories)] += 1
    return counts

expected = trials / len(categories)
counts_a = run_once()
counts_b = run_once()
chi2 = sum((o - expected) ** 2 / expected for o in counts_a.values())
result = {"title": title, "entries": entries, "trials": trials,
          "categories": len(categories), "seed": seed,
          "counts": counts_a, "chi_square": round(chi2, 3)}
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Catalog: " + title)
print("Entries (published, approximate): %d" % entries)
print("Spot-check sample: %d entries across %d categories (fixed seed %d)" % (trials, len(categories), seed))
print("Category counts: " + json.dumps(counts_a, sort_keys=True))
print("Expected per category: %.2f entries" % expected)
print("Chi-square: %.3f (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)" % chi2)
print("Spot-check note: " + claimed_note)
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.")

Demo output

Catalog: UN Comtrade
Entries (published, approximate): 200
Spot-check sample: 25 entries across 4 categories (fixed seed 1000)
Category counts: {"exports": 4, "imports": 9, "re-exports": 3, "transit": 9}
Expected per category: 6.25 entries
Chi-square: 4.920 (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)
Spot-check note: As published: official merchandise trade statistics reported by more than 200 economies, from 1962 to the present, free to use with citation.
SHA-256 run 1: 1e08198318b6e774f02ad666bfbc76e785d99a0ad7c5852a1b2910dad48e199a
SHA-256 run 2: 1e08198318b6e774f02ad666bfbc76e785d99a0ad7c5852a1b2910dad48e199a
Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.
Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.

The WTO Trade Statistics Database Rank 2 · simple

Evidence: design-study Design study: this record describes an original Signature design for The WTO Trade Statistics Database, a design study that presents no public fact as established and no design as a record.

The WTO trade statistics service gathers the trade data that WTO members notify to the organization, with dashboards, data downloads, and the Tariff and Trade Data platform. It is the WTO's own public window onto member trade.

History

The World Trade Organization was established in 1995 as the successor to the GATT, and its statistics work descends from GATT-era trade reporting. The WTO launched its integrated statistics portal and later the Tariff and Trade Data platform, consolidating the Integrated Data Base, the Consolidated Tariff Schedules, and the Analytical Database into one service. The history above is the published history: dates and owners as the database's own publications state them, with later developments carried in the public-record section below. A reader who wants the full chronology should read the database's own about page and its published documentation, which are named in the verification path. The dates in this record are the dates the operator publishes; where the operator gives only a decade or an era, this record gives the decade or the era rather than inventing a year.

Public record

The WTO trade statistics service gathers the trade data that WTO members notify to the organization, with dashboards, data downloads, and the Tariff and Trade Data platform. It is the WTO's own public window onto member trade. It covers more than 150 economies, with tariff and import data from 1996 onward for many of them. The Tariff and Trade Data platform is free to browse. Some detailed download files are restricted to member governments and authorized international organizations, which this record states plainly; no free public API is published for the full restricted downloads. That is the public record in outline. In detail, the record holds the subject in the form the operator publishes it: entries, classifications, and search tools, with the operator's own documentation as the guide to what each field means. The license and access terms above are the whole of the terms this record states, because they are the whole of the terms the publisher has published: where a term is unpublished, this record says so rather than guessing. Nothing in this record is a copy of the database itself: the catalog entry describes the database, and the database itself is reached through the links and the verification path.

Signature version

The Signature edition designs a WTO statistics companion: a plain-language guide to the member notifications, with each member's trade shown as a single readable page, a worked example of reading a bound tariff, and a timeline that shows how the notifications changed over the years. The design adds a teaching layer that explains bound versus applied tariffs in one page each. The design is original Signature work: it borrows the subject of the public database and nothing else, and it is labelled a design study in every place it appears, so that no reader mistakes it for an established public record. The design is judged as a design: whether it teaches the subject plainly, whether it presents the subject honestly, and whether a reader who follows it learns the subject rather than a copy of the database. Science proof

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. The demo runs that audit twice with the same seed and hashes both results, so a fresh run verifies the arithmetic exactly. The audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic; it does not query the live database and it proves nothing about the live holdings, which must be checked against the published source itself. The science of WTO statistics is the science of notification: a tariff is a legal commitment as well as a number, and the database must keep the bound rate, the applied rate, and the import flow side by side so the three can be compared. In plain words, the demo draws 25 entries at random with a fixed seed across 4 categories, so each category should receive about 6.25 entries by chance alone. The demo's simulated run gave counts {"commitments": 8, "imports": 6, "notifications": 5, "tariffs": 6} and a chi-square of 0.760. A chi-square near zero is exactly what a flat random draw produces; a large chi-square would need a reason, and the audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic, not that the live database is balanced.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic: expected per category E = 25 / 4 = 6.25. The demo's simulated counts were {"commitments": 8, "imports": 6, "notifications": 5, "tariffs": 6}. Chi-square = the sum over the 4 categories of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected, which comes to 0.760. The demo runs the sample twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

Scientific equation

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. The demo runs that audit twice with the same seed and hashes both results, so a fresh run verifies the arithmetic exactly. Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math
import random

title = 'The WTO Trade Statistics Database'
entries = 150
categories = ['tariffs', 'imports', 'commitments', 'notifications']
trials = 25
seed = 1013
claimed_note = 'As published: official tariff and import data for more than 150 economies, with annual data from 1996 onward for many of them.'

def run_once():
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    counts = {c: 0 for c in categories}
    for _ in range(trials):
        counts[rng.choice(categories)] += 1
    return counts

expected = trials / len(categories)
counts_a = run_once()
counts_b = run_once()
chi2 = sum((o - expected) ** 2 / expected for o in counts_a.values())
result = {"title": title, "entries": entries, "trials": trials,
          "categories": len(categories), "seed": seed,
          "counts": counts_a, "chi_square": round(chi2, 3)}
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Catalog: " + title)
print("Entries (published, approximate): %d" % entries)
print("Spot-check sample: %d entries across %d categories (fixed seed %d)" % (trials, len(categories), seed))
print("Category counts: " + json.dumps(counts_a, sort_keys=True))
print("Expected per category: %.2f entries" % expected)
print("Chi-square: %.3f (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)" % chi2)
print("Spot-check note: " + claimed_note)
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.")

Entries (published, approximate): 150
Spot-check sample: 25 entries across 4 categories (fixed seed 1013)
Category counts: {"commitments": 8, "imports": 6, "notifications": 5, "tariffs": 6}
Expected per category: 6.25 entries
Chi-square: 0.760 (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)
Spot-check note: As published: official tariff and import data for more than 150 economies, with annual data from 1996 onward for many of them.
SHA-256 run 1: f4a670322f4e1da8283d6b7c6c21dbca2e8956ade0a96fb27b11102f22aa63f9
SHA-256 run 2: f4a670322f4e1da8283d6b7c6c21dbca2e8956ade0a96fb27b11102f22aa63f9
Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.

The World Bank WITS Rank 3 · simple

Evidence: design-study Design study: this record describes an original Signature design for The World Bank WITS, a design study that presents no public fact as established and no design as a record.

WITS, the World Integrated Trade Solution, is a joint trade database and software service of the World Bank, UNCTAD, the WTO, and the United Nations Statistics Division, built on top of Comtrade and tariff sources. It gives researchers one door into four agencies' trade data.

The question behind The World Bank WITS is a catalog question: what does this trade database hold, who keeps it, and how does a reader reach the public record inside it? WITS, the World Integrated Trade Solution, is a joint trade database and software service of the World Bank, UNCTAD, the WTO, and the United Nations Statistics Division, built on top of Comtrade and tariff sources. It gives researchers one door into four agencies' trade data. That is the whole of the question, and this record answers it in order: what the database is, where its public record lives, what license and access method govern it, and what the Signature edition designs beside it. The question matters because trade databases are the shelves of the public record: a reader who cannot find the shelf cannot check the fact, and a fact that cannot be checked is only a rumor with good manners. Cataloguing the catalogues is therefore ordinary library work, done one database at a time, with the access terms stated plainly and nothing invented where the publisher has published nothing. A reader who opens The World Bank WITS is looking for world integrated trade data: the entries, the records, and the classifications that make up the public record of world integrated trade data. The database answers by holding those records in one place, in one classification, with one search, instead of leaving them scattered across a hundred publisher sites and a thousand documents. That is the service a trade database performs: it gathers, it classifies, it keeps the record searchable, and it lets a reader ask a question and get an answer that can be cited. The public record inside it is described in this entry in full: what information exists, where it lives, what license covers it, and how a reader reaches it, whether through a website, a download, or a published endpoint. Where no public endpoint is published, this record says so plainly, because an honest catalog never invents a door that is not there. The Signature version of The World Bank WITS is a separate matter, and it is kept separate in this record. The public edition describes what exists; the Signature edition designs what could be built beside it: an original Signature design that teaches, presents, or re-organizes the same subject without copying the public database and without presenting a design as an established record. The two editions share a title and a subject, and they differ in exactly that way: one reports, the other designs. This record therefore serves two readers at once: the reader who wants the public facts about The World Bank WITS, and the reader who wants to see what a Signature edition of the same subject would look like. Both readers get the same honesty: the public facts are stated as published, the design is labelled a design, and the ceiling line at the end of every record states the generator's limit in plain words.

History

WITS was developed in the 2000s as a shared front end for the partner agencies' trade data. The partners added tariff and non-tariff modules over successive releases, and the service now publishes a single harmonized interface over the four agencies' sources. The history above is the published history: dates and owners as the database's own publications state them, with later developments carried in the public-record section below. A reader who wants the full chronology should read the database's own about page and its published documentation, which are named in the verification path. This record gives the shape of the story, not a day-by-day chronicle, and it says so where a date is approximate rather than exact. What matters for the catalog is not the day the database opened but the continuity: the record has been kept, the classification has been maintained, and the public can still reach it, which is why it belongs in this pair. The dates in this record are the dates the operator publishes; where the operator gives only a decade or an era, this record gives the decade or the era rather than inventing a year.

Public record

WITS, the World Integrated Trade Solution, is a joint trade database and software service of the World Bank, UNCTAD, the WTO, and the United Nations Statistics Division, built on top of Comtrade and tariff sources. It gives researchers one door into four agencies' trade data. It covers bilateral trade flows, tariffs, and non-tariff measures for most economies, with data from Comtrade, TRAINS, and the WTO Integrated Data Base. WITS is free to use on the web, with a downloadable desktop application and an API; registration is free. That is the public record in outline. In detail, the record holds the subject in the form the operator publishes it: entries, classifications, and search tools, with the operator's own documentation as the guide to what each field means. The license and access terms above are the whole of the terms this record states, because they are the whole of the terms the publisher has published: where a term is unpublished, this record says so rather than guessing. A reader who needs more than the outline should follow the verification path, which names the database's own site and documentation; the catalog entry is a map to the record, and the record itself lives where the operator keeps it. The public record is also a living record: the operator adds entries, corrects entries, and revises classifications, so the counts in this entry are the counts as published at the time of writing, and a reader should check the live database for the current figure. Nothing in this record is a copy of the database itself: the catalog entry describes the database, and the database itself is reached through the links and the verification path.

Methods

Open the database's own site from the verification path and read its about page to confirm the operator and the coverage; Search the database for one world integrated trade data entry you already know, and check that the entry you find matches what you expected; Read the license and access terms on the operator's own terms page, and note whether a public endpoint is published; View or download one sample of the data in the form the operator publishes it, and check its classification against this record's description; Run the demo program beside this record and compare its output with the stored output, to confirm the sampling statistics; Read the Signature design in the signature edition and note what it adds without copying the public database.

Signature version

The Signature edition designs a WITS companion: a single-page trade profile for any country pair, built from the same four sources, with the flows, the tariffs, and the non-tariff measures shown together and explained in plain sentences. The design adds a worked example of tracing one product from its tariff line to its trade flow. The design is original Signature work: it borrows the subject of the public database and nothing else, and it is labelled a design study in every place it appears, so that no reader mistakes it for an established public record. The design is judged as a design: whether it teaches the subject plainly, whether it presents the subject honestly, and whether a reader who follows it learns the subject rather than a copy of the database. The Signature edition keeps the same title and the same ladder rank as the public edition, so the two can be read side by side, and the difference between them is exactly the difference between reporting what exists and designing what could be built beside it.

Limits and cautions

The limits of this record are the limits of a catalog entry. It describes The World Bank WITS; it does not contain it, and it cannot answer a question that only the database itself can answer, such as the current value of a single entry or the current text of a single record. The counts and dates in this record are snapshots from the operator's publications at the time of writing, and a living database will have moved on; the record says where the live figure can be checked, and a reader should check it. Where the operator publishes no public endpoint or no public license, this record says so, and the absence is a fact about the operator's publishing, not a gap in the catalog. The Signature design beside this record is a design study, not an established public record, and it must not be cited as evidence about the real database. Finally, this record is one of twenty-four in its pair, and it is written to be read beside the other twenty-three: no single entry carries the whole subject, and the field guide index record exists to hold the map.

Science proof

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. The demo runs that audit twice with the same seed and hashes both results, so a fresh run verifies the arithmetic exactly. The audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic; it does not query the live database and it proves nothing about the live holdings, which must be checked against the published source itself. The science of WITS is the science of harmonization: four agencies publish overlapping data in different formats, and the service must map them onto one classification before any comparison is honest. In plain words, the demo draws 25 entries at random with a fixed seed across 4 categories, so each category should receive about 6.25 entries by chance alone. The demo's simulated run gave counts {"flows": 5, "non-tariff measures": 8, "partners": 7, "tariffs": 5} and a chi-square of 1.080. A chi-square near zero is exactly what a flat random draw produces; a large chi-square would need a reason, and the audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic, not that the live database is balanced.

Worked arithmetic: expected per category E = 25 / 4 = 6.25. The demo's simulated counts were {"flows": 5, "non-tariff measures": 8, "partners": 7, "tariffs": 5}. The demo runs the sample twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

It is the standard source for who trades how much with whom. It covers more than 180 economies with monthly, quarterly, and annual bilateral trade values. DOTS is a subscription product through the IMF eLibrary Data service. Some aggregate series are published free in IMF data releases. No free public API is published for the full database. Dataset

It covers more than 180 economies with monthly, quarterly, and annual bilateral trade values. The dataset behind this record is the published record of the database itself: the counts, the classifications, and the access terms stated above, taken from the operator's own publications and from the live database's own pages. Science proof

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. The demo runs that audit twice with the same seed and hashes both results, so a fresh run verifies the arithmetic exactly. The audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic; it does not query the live database and it proves nothing about the live holdings, which must be checked against the published source itself. The science of DOTS is the science of bilateral valuation: an export valued free on board and the same shipment valued with cost, insurance, and freight differ by the cost of moving it, and the database must keep the two valuations apart. In plain words, the demo draws 25 entries at random with a fixed seed across 4 categories, so each category should receive about 6.25 entries by chance alone. The demo's simulated run gave counts {"exports": 10, "imports": 7, "partners": 4, "years": 4} and a chi-square of 3.960. A chi-square near zero is exactly what a flat random draw produces; a large chi-square would need a reason, and the audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic, not that the live database is balanced.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic: expected per category E = 25 / 4 = 6.25. The demo's simulated counts were {"exports": 10, "imports": 7, "partners": 4, "years": 4}. Chi-square = the sum over the 4 categories of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected, which comes to 3.960. The demo runs the sample twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

χ² = Σ (O_i − E)² / E, E = trials/categories (stated, not machine-checked)

Worked arithmetic: expected per category E = 25 / 4 = 6.25. The demo's simulated counts were {"exports": 10, "imports": 7, "partners": 4, "years": 4}. Signature trade database design, compiled November 2026

Spec: filed at merge; Wiki: JAH Wiki entry filed at merge; Dictionary: Dictionary definition filed at merge.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math
import random

title = 'The IMF Direction of Trade Statistics'
entries = 180
categories = ['exports', 'imports', 'partners', 'years']
trials = 25
seed = 1039
claimed_note = 'As published: monthly, quarterly, and annual bilateral merchandise trade values for more than 180 economies, by subscription through the IMF eLibrary.'

def run_once():
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    counts = {c: 0 for c in categories}
    for _ in range(trials):
        counts[rng.choice(categories)] += 1
    return counts

expected = trials / len(categories)
counts_a = run_once()
counts_b = run_once()
chi2 = sum((o - expected) ** 2 / expected for o in counts_a.values())
result = {"title": title, "entries": entries, "trials": trials,
          "categories": len(categories), "seed": seed,
          "counts": counts_a, "chi_square": round(chi2, 3)}
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Catalog: " + title)
print("Entries (published, approximate): %d" % entries)
print("Spot-check sample: %d entries across %d categories (fixed seed %d)" % (trials, len(categories), seed))
print("Category counts: " + json.dumps(counts_a, sort_keys=True))
print("Expected per category: %.2f entries" % expected)
print("Chi-square: %.3f (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)" % chi2)
print("Spot-check note: " + claimed_note)
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.")

Demo output

Catalog: The IMF Direction of Trade Statistics
Entries (published, approximate): 180
Spot-check sample: 25 entries across 4 categories (fixed seed 1039)
Category counts: {"exports": 10, "imports": 7, "partners": 4, "years": 4}
Expected per category: 6.25 entries
Chi-square: 3.960 (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)
Spot-check note: As published: monthly, quarterly, and annual bilateral merchandise trade values for more than 180 economies, by subscription through the IMF eLibrary.
SHA-256 run 1: 3fc6cbc993a75ed38f148de5a54c1aa93e6b2893b149258eda357043494d7747
SHA-256 run 2: 3fc6cbc993a75ed38f148de5a54c1aa93e6b2893b149258eda357043494d7747
Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.
Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.

The OECD Trade in Value Added (TiVA) Rank 5 · simple

Evidence: design-study Design study: this record describes an original Signature design for The OECD Trade in Value Added (TiVA), a design study that presents no public fact as established and no design as a record.

Trade in Value Added (TiVA) is an OECD database that measures trade in value-added terms, showing how much of a country's exports was actually created at home and how much came from imported inputs. It answers the question that gross trade figures cannot: who really made the value in what is exported.

The question behind The OECD Trade in Value Added (TiVA) is a catalog question: what does this trade database hold, who keeps it, and how does a reader reach the public record inside it? Trade in Value Added (TiVA) is an OECD database that measures trade in value-added terms, showing how much of a country's exports was actually created at home and how much came from imported inputs. It answers the question that gross trade figures cannot: who really made the value in what is exported. That is the whole of the question, and this record answers it in order: what the database is, where its public record lives, what license and access method govern it, and what the Signature edition designs beside it. The question matters because trade databases are the shelves of the public record: a reader who cannot find the shelf cannot check the fact, and a fact that cannot be checked is only a rumor with good manners. Cataloguing the catalogues is therefore ordinary library work, done one database at a time, with the access terms stated plainly and nothing invented where the publisher has published nothing. A reader who opens The OECD Trade in Value Added (TiVA) is looking for trade in value-added terms: the entries, the records, and the classifications that make up the public record of trade in value-added terms. The database answers by holding those records in one place, in one classification, with one search, instead of leaving them scattered across a hundred publisher sites and a thousand documents. That is the service a trade database performs: it gathers, it classifies, it keeps the record searchable, and it lets a reader ask a question and get an answer that can be cited. The public record inside it is described in this entry in full: what information exists, where it lives, what license covers it, and how a reader reaches it, whether through a website, a download, or a published endpoint. Where no public endpoint is published, this record says so plainly, because an honest catalog never invents a door that is not there. The Signature version of The OECD Trade in Value Added (TiVA) is a separate matter, and it is kept separate in this record. The public edition describes what exists; the Signature edition designs what could be built beside it: an original Signature design that teaches, presents, or re-organizes the same subject without copying the public database and without presenting a design as an established record. The two editions share a title and a subject, and they differ in exactly that way: one reports, the other designs. This record therefore serves two readers at once: the reader who wants the public facts about The OECD Trade in Value Added (TiVA), and the reader who wants to see what a Signature edition of the same subject would look like. Both readers get the same honesty: the public facts are stated as published, the design is labelled a design, and the ceiling line at the end of every record states the generator's limit in plain words.

History

TiVA was first released in January 2013, built by the OECD and the WTO from national input-output tables. Successive releases expanded country and industry coverage and added indicators on services value added and on the origin of value in exports. The history above is the published history: dates and owners as the database's own publications state them, with later developments carried in the public-record section below. A reader who wants the full chronology should read the database's own about page and its published documentation, which are named in the verification path. This record gives the shape of the story, not a day-by-day chronicle, and it says so where a date is approximate rather than exact. What matters for the catalog is not the day the database opened but the continuity: the record has been kept, the classification has been maintained, and the public can still reach it, which is why it belongs in this pair. The dates in this record are the dates the operator publishes; where the operator gives only a decade or an era, this record gives the decade or the era rather than inventing a year.

Public record

Trade in Value Added (TiVA) is an OECD database that measures trade in value-added terms, showing how much of a country's exports was actually created at home and how much came from imported inputs. It answers the question that gross trade figures cannot: who really made the value in what is exported. It covers more than 70 economies and dozens of industries, with indicators for domestic and foreign value added in exports. TiVA is free through the OECD Data Explorer and as downloadable files; the underlying input-output tables are published alongside the indicators. That is the public record in outline. In detail, the record holds the subject in the form the operator publishes it: entries, classifications, and search tools, with the operator's own documentation as the guide to what each field means. The license and access terms above are the whole of the terms this record states, because they are the whole of the terms the publisher has published: where a term is unpublished, this record says so rather than guessing. A reader who needs more than the outline should follow the verification path, which names the database's own site and documentation; the catalog entry is a map to the record, and the record itself lives where the operator keeps it. The public record is also a living record: the operator adds entries, corrects entries, and revises classifications, so the counts in this entry are the counts as published at the time of writing, and a reader should check the live database for the current figure. Nothing in this record is a copy of the database itself: the catalog entry describes the database, and the database itself is reached through the links and the verification path.

Methods

Open the database's own site from the verification path and read its about page to confirm the operator and the coverage; Search the database for one trade in value-added terms entry you already know, and check that the entry you find matches what you expected; Read the license and access terms on the operator's own terms page, and note whether a public endpoint is published; View or download one sample of the data in the form the operator publishes it, and check its classification against this record's description; Run the demo program beside this record and compare its output with the stored output, to confirm the sampling statistics; Read the Signature design in the signature edition and note what it adds without copying the public database.

Signature version

The Signature edition designs a TiVA companion: a value-chain story for one exported product, tracing each share of its value back to the country and industry that created it, with the shares drawn as a single stacked picture and explained in plain sentences. The design adds a comparison view for two countries' export baskets. The design is original Signature work: it borrows the subject of the public database and nothing else, and it is labelled a design study in every place it appears, so that no reader mistakes it for an established public record. The design is judged as a design: whether it teaches the subject plainly, whether it presents the subject honestly, and whether a reader who follows it learns the subject rather than a copy of the database. The Signature edition keeps the same title and the same ladder rank as the public edition, so the two can be read side by side, and the difference between them is exactly the difference between reporting what exists and designing what could be built beside it.

Limits and cautions

The limits of this record are the limits of a catalog entry. It describes The OECD Trade in Value Added (TiVA); it does not contain it, and it cannot answer a question that only the database itself can answer, such as the current value of a single entry or the current text of a single record. The counts and dates in this record are snapshots from the operator's publications at the time of writing, and a living database will have moved on; the record says where the live figure can be checked, and a reader should check it. Where the operator publishes no public endpoint or no public license, this record says so, and the absence is a fact about the operator's publishing, not a gap in the catalog. The Signature design beside this record is a design study, not an established public record, and it must not be cited as evidence about the real database. Finally, this record is one of twenty-four in its pair, and it is written to be read beside the other twenty-three: no single entry carries the whole subject, and the field guide index record exists to hold the map.

Generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Science proof

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. The demo runs that audit twice with the same seed and hashes both results, so a fresh run verifies the arithmetic exactly. The audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic; it does not query the live database and it proves nothing about the live holdings, which must be checked against the published source itself. The science of Census trade data is the science of customs records: every shipment is a customs filing with a value, a commodity code, and a port, and the statistics are the sum of those filings, checked against the filings of the partner country. In plain words, the demo draws 25 entries at random with a fixed seed across 5 categories, so each category should receive about 5.00 entries by chance alone. The demo's simulated run gave counts {"balance": 5, "commodities": 4, "exports": 4, "imports": 9, "ports": 3} and a chi-square of 4.400. A chi-square near zero is exactly what a flat random draw produces; a large chi-square would need a reason, and the audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic, not that the live database is balanced.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic: expected per category E = 25 / 5 = 5.00. The demo's simulated counts were {"balance": 5, "commodities": 4, "exports": 4, "imports": 9, "ports": 3}. Chi-square = the sum over the 5 categories of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected, which comes to 4.400. The demo runs the sample twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

χ² = Σ (O_i − E)² / E, E = trials/categories (stated, not machine-checked)

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. Evidence links

Spec: filed at merge; Wiki: JAH Wiki entry filed at merge; Dictionary: Dictionary definition filed at merge.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math
import random

title = 'The US Census Bureau Trade Data'
entries = 240
categories = ['exports', 'imports', 'balance', 'ports', 'commodities']
trials = 25
seed = 1065
claimed_note = 'As published: official United States merchandise trade statistics by commodity, country, and port, monthly and annual, free through USA Trade Online.'

def run_once():
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    counts = {c: 0 for c in categories}
    for _ in range(trials):
        counts[rng.choice(categories)] += 1
    return counts

expected = trials / len(categories)
counts_a = run_once()
counts_b = run_once()
chi2 = sum((o - expected) ** 2 / expected for o in counts_a.values())
result = {"title": title, "entries": entries, "trials": trials,
          "categories": len(categories), "seed": seed,
          "counts": counts_a, "chi_square": round(chi2, 3)}
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Catalog: " + title)
print("Entries (published, approximate): %d" % entries)
print("Spot-check sample: %d entries across %d categories (fixed seed %d)" % (trials, len(categories), seed))
print("Category counts: " + json.dumps(counts_a, sort_keys=True))
print("Expected per category: %.2f entries" % expected)
print("Chi-square: %.3f (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)" % chi2)
print("Spot-check note: " + claimed_note)
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.")

Demo output

Catalog: The US Census Bureau Trade Data
Entries (published, approximate): 240
Spot-check sample: 25 entries across 5 categories (fixed seed 1065)
Category counts: {"balance": 5, "commodities": 4, "exports": 4, "imports": 9, "ports": 3}
Expected per category: 5.00 entries
Chi-square: 4.400 (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)
Spot-check note: As published: official United States merchandise trade statistics by commodity, country, and port, monthly and annual, free through USA Trade Online.
SHA-256 run 1: ddea1aa65470b7159fa8f8ec90925c4e1702e97eb62176c04ff7f4b389c8055c
SHA-256 run 2: ddea1aa65470b7159fa8f8ec90925c4e1702e97eb62176c04ff7f4b389c8055c
Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.
Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.
Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.

Eurostat Comext Rank 7 · simple

Evidence: design-study Design study: this record describes an original Signature design for Eurostat Comext, a design study that presents no public fact as established and no design as a record.

Comext is the European Union's reference database for trade in goods between EU member states and with the rest of the world, published by Eurostat. It is the source for every published figure on EU goods trade.

The question behind Eurostat Comext is a catalog question: what does this trade database hold, who keeps it, and how does a reader reach the public record inside it? Comext is the European Union's reference database for trade in goods between EU member states and with the rest of the world, published by Eurostat. It is the source for every published figure on EU goods trade. That is the whole of the question, and this record answers it in order: what the database is, where its public record lives, what license and access method govern it, and what the Signature edition designs beside it. The question matters because trade databases are the shelves of the public record: a reader who cannot find the shelf cannot check the fact, and a fact that cannot be checked is only a rumor with good manners. Cataloguing the catalogues is therefore ordinary library work, done one database at a time, with the access terms stated plainly and nothing invented where the publisher has published nothing. A reader who opens Eurostat Comext is looking for European Union trade in goods: the entries, the records, and the classifications that make up the public record of European Union trade in goods. The database answers by holding those records in one place, in one classification, with one search, instead of leaving them scattered across a hundred publisher sites and a thousand documents. That is the service a trade database performs: it gathers, it classifies, it keeps the record searchable, and it lets a reader ask a question and get an answer that can be cited. The public record inside it is described in this entry in full: what information exists, where it lives, what license covers it, and how a reader reaches it, whether through a website, a download, or a published endpoint. History

Comext descends from EU trade reporting that began with the common market; the modern dissemination database is published through the Eurostat portal. The database absorbed the Intrastat system for intra-EU trade and the Extrastat system for trade with non-members, and it now publishes monthly data within weeks of the reference month. The history above is the published history: dates and owners as the database's own publications state them, with later developments carried in the public-record section below. A reader who wants the full chronology should read the database's own about page and its published documentation, which are named in the verification path. This record gives the shape of the story, not a day-by-day chronicle, and it says so where a date is approximate rather than exact. What matters for the catalog is not the day the database opened but the continuity: the record has been kept, the classification has been maintained, and the public can still reach it, which is why it belongs in this pair. The dates in this record are the dates the operator publishes; where the operator gives only a decade or an era, this record gives the decade or the era rather than inventing a year.

Public record

Comext is the European Union's reference database for trade in goods between EU member states and with the rest of the world, published by Eurostat. It is the source for every published figure on EU goods trade. It covers trade in goods for all EU member states and EFTA countries, monthly and annual, in Combined Nomenclature detail. Comext data are free through the Eurostat dissemination service and its API; bulk downloads are published in the Eurostat bulk download facility. That is the public record in outline. In detail, the record holds the subject in the form the operator publishes it: entries, classifications, and search tools, with the operator's own documentation as the guide to what each field means. The license and access terms above are the whole of the terms this record states, because they are the whole of the terms the publisher has published: where a term is unpublished, this record says so rather than guessing. Nothing in this record is a copy of the database itself: the catalog entry describes the database, and the database itself is reached through the links and the verification path.

History

The Atlas descends from Global Trade Information Services, founded in the 1990s; S&P Global acquired the service and continues it under the Global Trade Atlas name. The service expanded from United States import data to worldwide coverage, and it added tools for searching bills of lading and company trade activity. The history above is the published history: dates and owners as the database's own publications state them, with later developments carried in the public-record section below. A reader who wants the full chronology should read the database's own about page and its published documentation, which are named in the verification path. This record gives the shape of the story, not a day-by-day chronicle, and it says so where a date is approximate rather than exact. What matters for the catalog is not the day the database opened but the continuity: the record has been kept, the classification has been maintained, and the public can still reach it, which is why it belongs in this pair. The dates in this record are the dates the operator publishes; where the operator gives only a decade or an era, this record gives the decade or the era rather than inventing a year.

Public record

The Global Trade Atlas, from S&P Global Market Intelligence, is a subscription database of official trade statistics for more than 60 reporting countries, with import and export detail by commodity and partner. It covers official trade statistics for dozens of reporting countries, with monthly and annual flows by commodity and partner. The Global Trade Atlas is a paid subscription service. No free public API is published for the full database. That is the public record in outline. In detail, the record holds the subject in the form the operator publishes it: entries, classifications, and search tools, with the operator's own documentation as the guide to what each field means. The license and access terms above are the whole of the terms this record states, because they are the whole of the terms the publisher has published: where a term is unpublished, this record says so rather than guessing. A reader who needs more than the outline should follow the verification path, which names the database's own site and documentation; the catalog entry is a map to the record, and the record itself lives where the operator keeps it. The public record is also a living record: the operator adds entries, corrects entries, and revises classifications, so the counts in this entry are the counts as published at the time of writing, and a reader should check the live database for the current figure. Nothing in this record is a copy of the database itself: the catalog entry describes the database, and the database itself is reached through the links and the verification path.

Methods

Open the database's own site from the verification path and read its about page to confirm the operator and the coverage; Search the database for one official trade statistics for more than 60 countries entry you already know, and check that the entry you find matches what you expected; Read the license and access terms on the operator's own terms page, and note whether a public endpoint is published; View or download one sample of the data in the form the operator publishes it, and check its classification against this record's description; Run the demo program beside this record and compare its output with the stored output, to confirm the sampling statistics; Read the Signature design in the signature edition and note what it adds without copying the public database.

Signature version

The Signature edition designs a Trade Atlas companion: a single-country trade profile that shows the country's top commodities and partners on one page, with each flow explained in plain words and a worked example of tracing one shipment from its commodity code to its partner. The design adds a plain-language guide to reading official trade statistics. The design is original Signature work: it borrows the subject of the public database and nothing else, and it is labelled a design study in every place it appears, so that no reader mistakes it for an established public record. The design is judged as a design: whether it teaches the subject plainly, whether it presents the subject honestly, and whether a reader who follows it learns the subject rather than a copy of the database. The Signature edition keeps the same title and the same ladder rank as the public edition, so the two can be read side by side, and the difference between them is exactly the difference between reporting what exists and designing what could be built beside it.

Limits and cautions

The limits of this record are the limits of a catalog entry. It describes The Global Trade Atlas; it does not contain it, and it cannot answer a question that only the database itself can answer, such as the current value of a single entry or the current text of a single record. The counts and dates in this record are snapshots from the operator's publications at the time of writing, and a living database will have moved on; the record says where the live figure can be checked, and a reader should check it. Where the operator publishes no public endpoint or no public license, this record says so, and the absence is a fact about the operator's publishing, not a gap in the catalog. The Signature design beside this record is a design study, not an established public record, and it must not be cited as evidence about the real database. Finally, this record is one of twenty-four in its pair, and it is written to be read beside the other twenty-three: no single entry carries the whole subject, and the field guide index record exists to hold the map.

Science proof

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. The demo runs that audit twice with the same seed and hashes both results, so a fresh run verifies the arithmetic exactly. The audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic; it does not query the live database and it proves nothing about the live holdings, which must be checked against the published source itself. The science of the Atlas is the science of harmonizing official statistics: each reporting country publishes its trade in its own format and classification, and the service must map them onto one comparable structure. In plain words, the demo draws 25 entries at random with a fixed seed across 4 categories, so each category should receive about 6.25 entries by chance alone. The demo's simulated run gave counts {"commodities": 11, "exports": 5, "imports": 7, "partners": 2} and a chi-square of 6.840. A chi-square near zero is exactly what a flat random draw produces; a large chi-square would need a reason, and the audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic, not that the live database is balanced.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic: expected per category E = 25 / 4 = 6.25. The demo's simulated counts were {"commodities": 11, "exports": 5, "imports": 7, "partners": 2}. Chi-square = the sum over the 4 categories of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected, which comes to 6.840. The demo runs the sample twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

χ² = Σ (O_i − E)² / E, E = trials/categories (stated, not machine-checked)

Scientific equation

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. The demo runs that audit twice with the same seed and hashes both results, so a fresh run verifies the arithmetic exactly. The audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic; it does not query the live database and it proves nothing about the live holdings, which must be checked against the published source itself. The science of the Atlas is the science of harmonizing official statistics: each reporting country publishes its trade in its own format and classification, and the service must map them onto one comparable structure. Worked arithmetic: expected per category E = 25 / 4 = 6.25. The demo's simulated counts were {"commodities": 11, "exports": 5, "imports": 7, "partners": 2}. Evidence links

Spec: filed at merge; Wiki: JAH Wiki entry filed at merge; Dictionary: Dictionary definition filed at merge.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math
import random

title = 'The Global Trade Atlas'
entries = 60
categories = ['imports', 'exports', 'commodities', 'partners']
trials = 25
seed = 1091
claimed_note = 'As published: official trade statistics for more than 60 reporting countries, by paid subscription.'

def run_once():
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    counts = {c: 0 for c in categories}
    for _ in range(trials):
        counts[rng.choice(categories)] += 1
    return counts

expected = trials / len(categories)
counts_a = run_once()
counts_b = run_once()
chi2 = sum((o - expected) ** 2 / expected for o in counts_a.values())
result = {"title": title, "entries": entries, "trials": trials,
          "categories": len(categories), "seed": seed,
          "counts": counts_a, "chi_square": round(chi2, 3)}
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Catalog: " + title)
print("Entries (published, approximate): %d" % entries)
print("Spot-check sample: %d entries across %d categories (fixed seed %d)" % (trials, len(categories), seed))
print("Category counts: " + json.dumps(counts_a, sort_keys=True))
print("Expected per category: %.2f entries" % expected)
print("Chi-square: %.3f (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)" % chi2)
print("Spot-check note: " + claimed_note)
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.")

Demo output

Catalog: The Global Trade Atlas
Entries (published, approximate): 60
Spot-check sample: 25 entries across 4 categories (fixed seed 1091)
Category counts: {"commodities": 11, "exports": 5, "imports": 7, "partners": 2}
Expected per category: 6.25 entries
Chi-square: 6.840 (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)
Spot-check note: As published: official trade statistics for more than 60 reporting countries, by paid subscription.
SHA-256 run 1: 422238c04b00cc401bb42a135a09d1da6cf04a0f3159bdac501b1f74eaeb4195
SHA-256 run 2: 422238c04b00cc401bb42a135a09d1da6cf04a0f3159bdac501b1f74eaeb4195
Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.
Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.
Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.

The UNCTADstat Database Rank 9 · compounded

UNCTADstat is the statistical portal of the United Nations Conference on Trade and Development, publishing trade, investment, and development indicators for nearly all economies.

The question behind The UNCTADstat Database is a catalog question: what does this trade database hold, who keeps it, and how does a reader reach the public record inside it? UNCTADstat is the statistical portal of the United Nations Conference on Trade and Development, publishing trade, investment, and development indicators for nearly all economies. That is the whole of the question, and this record answers it in order: what the database is, where its public record lives, what license and access method govern it, and what the Signature edition designs beside it. The question matters because trade databases are the shelves of the public record: a reader who cannot find the shelf cannot check the fact, and a fact that cannot be checked is only a rumor with good manners. Cataloguing the catalogues is therefore ordinary library work, done one database at a time, with the access terms stated plainly and nothing invented where the publisher has published nothing. A reader who opens The UNCTADstat Database is looking for trade, investment, and development indicators: the entries, the records, and the classifications that make up the public record of trade, investment, and development indicators. The database answers by holding those records in one place, in one classification, with one search, instead of leaving them scattered across a hundred publisher sites and a thousand documents. That is the service a trade database performs: it gathers, it classifies, it keeps the record searchable, and it lets a reader ask a question and get an answer that can be cited. The public record inside it is described in this entry in full: what information exists, where it lives, what license covers it, and how a reader reaches it, whether through a website, a download, or a published endpoint. The two editions share a title and a subject, and they differ in exactly that way: one reports, the other designs. This record therefore serves two readers at once: the reader who wants the public facts about The UNCTADstat Database, and the reader who wants to see what a Signature edition of the same subject would look like. Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math
import random

title = 'The UNCTADstat Database'
entries = 190
categories = ['trade', 'investment', 'transport', 'development']
trials = 25
seed = 1104
claimed_note = 'As published: trade, investment, and development indicators for nearly all economies, free on the web.'

def run_once():
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    counts = {c: 0 for c in categories}
    for _ in range(trials):
        counts[rng.choice(categories)] += 1
    return counts

expected = trials / len(categories)
counts_a = run_once()
counts_b = run_once()
chi2 = sum((o - expected) ** 2 / expected for o in counts_a.values())
result = {"title": title, "entries": entries, "trials": trials,
          "categories": len(categories), "seed": seed,
          "counts": counts_a, "chi_square": round(chi2, 3)}
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Catalog: " + title)
print("Entries (published, approximate): %d" % entries)
print("Spot-check sample: %d entries across %d categories (fixed seed %d)" % (trials, len(categories), seed))
print("Category counts: " + json.dumps(counts_a, sort_keys=True))
print("Expected per category: %.2f entries" % expected)
print("Chi-square: %.3f (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)" % chi2)
print("Spot-check note: " + claimed_note)
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.")
