The database answers by holding those records in one place, in one classification, with one search, instead of leaving them scattered across a hundred publisher sites and a thousand documents. That is the service a trade database performs: it gathers, it classifies, it keeps the record searchable, and it lets a reader ask a question and get an answer that can be cited. The public record inside it is described in this entry in full: what information exists, where it lives, what license covers it, and how a reader reaches it, whether through a website, a download, or a published endpoint. Where no public endpoint is published, this record says so plainly, because an honest catalog never invents a door that is not there. The Signature version of The ITC Trade Map is a separate matter, and it is kept separate in this record. The public edition describes what exists; the Signature edition designs what could be built beside it: an original Signature design that teaches, presents, or re-organizes the same subject without copying the public database and without presenting a design as an established record. The two editions share a title and a subject, and they differ in exactly that way: one reports, the other designs. This record therefore serves two readers at once: the reader who wants the public facts about The ITC Trade Map, and the reader who wants to see what a Signature edition of the same subject would look like. Both readers get the same honesty: the public facts are stated as published, the design is labelled a design, and the ceiling line at the end of every record states the generator's limit in plain words.

History

The International Trade Centre was established in 1964 as a joint agency of the WTO and the United Nations; Trade Map is its flagship statistics service. Trade Map grew from a CD-ROM product into a web service with company-level trade data and market-access tools. The history above is the published history: dates and owners as the database's own publications state them, with later developments carried in the public-record section below. A reader who wants the full chronology should read the database's own about page and its published documentation, which are named in the verification path. This record gives the shape of the story, not a day-by-day chronicle, and it says so where a date is approximate rather than exact. What matters for the catalog is not the day the database opened but the continuity: the record has been kept, the classification has been maintained, and the public can still reach it, which is why it belongs in this pair. The dates in this record are the dates the operator publishes; where the operator gives only a decade or an era, this record gives the decade or the era rather than inventing a year.

Public record

Trade Map, from the International Trade Centre, is a database of international trade statistics for more than 220 countries and territories, built for exporters finding markets. It shows what each country buys and sells, and from whom. It covers trade flows for more than 220 countries and territories, with monthly, quarterly, and annual data by product and partner. Trade Map is free for users in developing countries and for registered users elsewhere; a public API is offered to registered users. That is the public record in outline. In detail, the record holds the subject in the form the operator publishes it: entries, classifications, and search tools, with the operator's own documentation as the guide to what each field means. The license and access terms above are the whole of the terms this record states, because they are the whole of the terms the publisher has published: where a term is unpublished, this record says so rather than guessing. A reader who needs more than the outline should follow the verification path, which names the database's own site and documentation; the catalog entry is a map to the record, and the record itself lives where the operator keeps it. The public record is also a living record: the operator adds entries, corrects entries, and revises classifications, so the counts in this entry are the counts as published at the time of writing, and a reader should check the live database for the current figure. Nothing in this record is a copy of the database itself: the catalog entry describes the database, and the database itself is reached through the links and the verification path.

Signature version

The Signature edition designs a Trade Map companion: a market-finder page for one exporter, showing the largest importers of its product, the tariffs they charge, and the competitors already selling there, each explained in plain words. The design adds a worked example of choosing one new market from the data. The design is original Signature work: it borrows the subject of the public database and nothing else, and it is labelled a design study in every place it appears, so that no reader mistakes it for an established public record. The design is judged as a design: whether it teaches the subject plainly, whether it presents the subject honestly, and whether a reader who follows it learns the subject rather than a copy of the database. Limits and cautions

The limits of this record are the limits of a catalog entry. It describes The ITC Trade Map; it does not contain it, and it cannot answer a question that only the database itself can answer, such as the current value of a single entry or the current text of a single record. The counts and dates in this record are snapshots from the operator's publications at the time of writing, and a living database will have moved on; the record says where the live figure can be checked, and a reader should check it. Where the operator publishes no public endpoint or no public license, this record says so, and the absence is a fact about the operator's publishing, not a gap in the catalog. The Signature design beside this record is a design study, not an established public record, and it must not be cited as evidence about the real database. Finally, this record is one of twenty-four in its pair, and it is written to be read beside the other twenty-three: no single entry carries the whole subject, and the field guide index record exists to hold the map.

Science proof

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. The demo runs that audit twice with the same seed and hashes both results, so a fresh run verifies the arithmetic exactly. The audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic; it does not query the live database and it proves nothing about the live holdings, which must be checked against the published source itself. The science of Trade Map is the science of market questions: an exporter needs to know not only what a market imports, but how fast that import is growing and who already supplies it, and the database must answer all three at once. In plain words, the demo draws 25 entries at random with a fixed seed across 4 categories, so each category should receive about 6.25 entries by chance alone. The demo's simulated run gave counts {"flows": 6, "markets": 3, "partners": 7, "products": 9} and a chi-square of 3.000. A chi-square near zero is exactly what a flat random draw produces; a large chi-square would need a reason, and the audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic, not that the live database is balanced.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic: expected per category E = 25 / 4 = 6.25. The demo's simulated counts were {"flows": 6, "markets": 3, "partners": 7, "products": 9}. Chi-square = the sum over the 4 categories of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected, which comes to 3.000. The demo runs the sample twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

χ² = Σ (O_i − E)² / E, E = trials/categories (stated, not machine-checked)

Scientific equation

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math
import random

title = 'The ITC Trade Map'
entries = 220
categories = ['flows', 'markets', 'products', 'partners']
trials = 25
seed = 1117
claimed_note = 'As published: trade statistics for more than 220 countries and territories, free for users in developing countries and registered users elsewhere.'

def run_once():
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    counts = {c: 0 for c in categories}
    for _ in range(trials):
        counts[rng.choice(categories)] += 1
    return counts

expected = trials / len(categories)
counts_a = run_once()
counts_b = run_once()
chi2 = sum((o - expected) ** 2 / expected for o in counts_a.values())
result = {"title": title, "entries": entries, "trials": trials,
          "categories": len(categories), "seed": seed,
          "counts": counts_a, "chi_square": round(chi2, 3)}
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Catalog: " + title)
print("Entries (published, approximate): %d" % entries)
print("Spot-check sample: %d entries across %d categories (fixed seed %d)" % (trials, len(categories), seed))
print("Category counts: " + json.dumps(counts_a, sort_keys=True))
print("Expected per category: %.2f entries" % expected)
print("Chi-square: %.3f (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)" % chi2)
print("Spot-check note: " + claimed_note)
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.")

Demo output

Catalog: The ITC Trade Map
Entries (published, approximate): 220
Spot-check sample: 25 entries across 4 categories (fixed seed 1117)
Category counts: {"flows": 6, "markets": 3, "partners": 7, "products": 9}
Expected per category: 6.25 entries
Chi-square: 3.000 (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)
Spot-check note: As published: trade statistics for more than 220 countries and territories, free for users in developing countries and registered users elsewhere.
SHA-256 run 1: 50b46488d002bacba3dd8d3cbb5815191c94373a3a4c396b64c878e1cb256fdd
SHA-256 run 2: 50b46488d002bacba3dd8d3cbb5815191c94373a3a4c396b64c878e1cb256fdd
Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.
Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.

The WCO Harmonized System Database Rank 12 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Design study: this record describes an original Signature design for The WCO Harmonized System Database, a design study that presents no public fact as established and no design as a record.

The Harmonized System database, maintained by the World Customs Organization, is the master classification of traded goods: the six-digit codes that customs administrations worldwide use to classify products.

The question behind The WCO Harmonized System Database is a catalog question: what does this trade database hold, who keeps it, and how does a reader reach the public record inside it? The Harmonized System database, maintained by the World Customs Organization, is the master classification of traded goods: the six-digit codes that customs administrations worldwide use to classify products. That is the whole of the question, and this record answers it in order: what the database is, where its public record lives, what license and access method govern it, and what the Signature edition designs beside it. The question matters because trade databases are the shelves of the public record: a reader who cannot find the shelf cannot check the fact, and a fact that cannot be checked is only a rumor with good manners. Cataloguing the catalogues is therefore ordinary library work, done one database at a time, with the access terms stated plainly and nothing invented where the publisher has published nothing. A reader who opens The WCO Harmonized System Database is looking for the classification of traded goods: the entries, the records, and the classifications that make up the public record of the classification of traded goods. The database answers by holding those records in one place, in one classification, with one search, instead of leaving them scattered across a hundred publisher sites and a thousand documents. That is the service a trade database performs: it gathers, it classifies, it keeps the record searchable, and it lets a reader ask a question and get an answer that can be cited. The public record inside it is described in this entry in full: what information exists, where it lives, what license covers it, and how a reader reaches it, whether through a website, a download, or a published endpoint. Where no public endpoint is published, this record says so plainly, because an honest catalog never invents a door that is not there. The Signature version of The WCO Harmonized System Database is a separate matter, and it is kept separate in this record. The public edition describes what exists; the Signature edition designs what could be built beside it: an original Signature design that teaches, presents, or re-organizes the same subject without copying the public database and without presenting a design as an established record. The two editions share a title and a subject, and they differ in exactly that way: one reports, the other designs. This record therefore serves two readers at once: the reader who wants the public facts about The WCO Harmonized System Database, and the reader who wants to see what a Signature edition of the same subject would look like. Both readers get the same honesty: the public facts are stated as published, the design is labelled a design, and the ceiling line at the end of every record states the generator's limit in plain words.

History

The Harmonized System was adopted in 1983 and entered into force in 1988; the WCO publishes a new edition every five years, most recently HS 2022. HS 2017 and HS 2022 added new subheadings for e-waste, drones, and other new products, and the WCO publishes correlation tables between editions. The history above is the published history: dates and owners as the database's own publications state them, with later developments carried in the public-record section below. A reader who wants the full chronology should read the database's own about page and its published documentation, which are named in the verification path. This record gives the shape of the story, not a day-by-day chronicle, and it says so where a date is approximate rather than exact. What matters for the catalog is not the day the database opened but the continuity: the record has been kept, the classification has been maintained, and the public can still reach it, which is why it belongs in this pair. The dates in this record are the dates the operator publishes; where the operator gives only a decade or an era, this record gives the decade or the era rather than inventing a year.

Public record

The Harmonized System database, maintained by the World Customs Organization, is the master classification of traded goods: the six-digit codes that customs administrations worldwide use to classify products. The HS covers more than 5,000 commodity groups in 99 chapters, used by more than 200 economies for customs tariffs and trade statistics. The HS nomenclature is published by the WCO; the full database is a licensed product, while the chapter structure and explanatory notes are described in WCO publications. No free public API is published for the full HS database. That is the public record in outline. In detail, the record holds the subject in the form the operator publishes it: entries, classifications, and search tools, with the operator's own documentation as the guide to what each field means. The license and access terms above are the whole of the terms this record states, because they are the whole of the terms the publisher has published: where a term is unpublished, this record says so rather than guessing. A reader who needs more than the outline should follow the verification path, which names the database's own site and documentation; the catalog entry is a map to the record, and the record itself lives where the operator keeps it. The public record is also a living record: the operator adds entries, corrects entries, and revises classifications, so the counts in this entry are the counts as published at the time of writing, and a reader should check the live database for the current figure. Nothing in this record is a copy of the database itself: the catalog entry describes the database, and the database itself is reached through the links and the verification path.

Methods

Open the database's own site from the verification path and read its about page to confirm the operator and the coverage; Search the database for one the classification of traded goods entry you already know, and check that the entry you find matches what you expected; Read the license and access terms on the operator's own terms page, and note whether a public endpoint is published; View or download one sample of the data in the form the operator publishes it, and check its classification against this record's description; Run the demo program beside this record and compare its output with the stored output, to confirm the sampling statistics; Read the Signature design in the signature edition and note what it adds without copying the public database.

Signature version

The Signature edition designs a Harmonized System companion: a plain-language guide to the 99 chapters, with each chapter introduced by the goods it holds, a worked example of classifying one product step by step, and a map of how a code changes between editions. The design adds a classroom exercise that teaches classification from the goods up. The design is original Signature work: it borrows the subject of the public database and nothing else, and it is labelled a design study in every place it appears, so that no reader mistakes it for an established public record. The design is judged as a design: whether it teaches the subject plainly, whether it presents the subject honestly, and whether a reader who follows it learns the subject rather than a copy of the database. The Signature edition keeps the same title and the same ladder rank as the public edition, so the two can be read side by side, and the difference between them is exactly the difference between reporting what exists and designing what could be built beside it.

Limits and cautions

The limits of this record are the limits of a catalog entry. It describes The WCO Harmonized System Database; it does not contain it, and it cannot answer a question that only the database itself can answer, such as the current value of a single entry or the current text of a single record. The counts and dates in this record are snapshots from the operator's publications at the time of writing, and a living database will have moved on; the record says where the live figure can be checked, and a reader should check it. Where the operator publishes no public endpoint or no public license, this record says so, and the absence is a fact about the operator's publishing, not a gap in the catalog. The Signature design beside this record is a design study, not an established public record, and it must not be cited as evidence about the real database. Finally, this record is one of twenty-four in its pair, and it is written to be read beside the other twenty-three: no single entry carries the whole subject, and the field guide index record exists to hold the map.

Dataset

The HS covers more than 5,000 commodity groups in 99 chapters, used by more than 200 economies for customs tariffs and trade statistics. The dataset behind this record is the published record of the database itself: the counts, the classifications, and the access terms stated above, taken from the operator's own publications and from the live database's own pages. It is not a dump of the database's contents, which would be a rebuild rather than a catalog entry, and this pair never rebuilds the databases it catalogues. The dataset is therefore small and honest: one entry per database, with the facts that let a reader find the database, understand what it holds, and reach it. The figures in this entry are the figures the operator publishes, and any figure that is an estimate is labelled as an estimate in the text. Generator

Science proof

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. The demo runs that audit twice with the same seed and hashes both results, so a fresh run verifies the arithmetic exactly. The audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic; it does not query the live database and it proves nothing about the live holdings, which must be checked against the published source itself. The science of the Harmonized System is the science of classification: a classification must put every traded good in exactly one place, and the six-digit code must stay stable enough for statistics while changing enough to cover new goods. In plain words, the demo draws 25 entries at random with a fixed seed across 4 categories, so each category should receive about 6.25 entries by chance alone. The demo's simulated run gave counts {"chapters": 5, "headings": 5, "notes": 10, "subheadings": 5} and a chi-square of 3.000. A chi-square near zero is exactly what a flat random draw produces; a large chi-square would need a reason, and the audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic, not that the live database is balanced.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic: expected per category E = 25 / 4 = 6.25. The demo's simulated counts were {"chapters": 5, "headings": 5, "notes": 10, "subheadings": 5}. Chi-square = the sum over the 4 categories of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected, which comes to 3.000. The demo runs the sample twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

χ² = Σ (O_i − E)² / E, E = trials/categories (stated, not machine-checked)

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math
import random

title = 'The WCO Harmonized System Database'
entries = 5000
categories = ['chapters', 'headings', 'subheadings', 'notes']
trials = 25
seed = 1143
claimed_note = 'As published: the Harmonized System classification, more than 5,000 commodity groups in 99 chapters, current edition HS 2022, licensed from the WCO.'

def run_once():
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    counts = {c: 0 for c in categories}
    for _ in range(trials):
        counts[rng.choice(categories)] += 1
    return counts

expected = trials / len(categories)
counts_a = run_once()
counts_b = run_once()
chi2 = sum((o - expected) ** 2 / expected for o in counts_a.values())
result = {"title": title, "entries": entries, "trials": trials,
          "categories": len(categories), "seed": seed,
          "counts": counts_a, "chi_square": round(chi2, 3)}
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Catalog: " + title)
print("Entries (published, approximate): %d" % entries)
print("Spot-check sample: %d entries across %d categories (fixed seed %d)" % (trials, len(categories), seed))
print("Category counts: " + json.dumps(counts_a, sort_keys=True))
print("Expected per category: %.2f entries" % expected)
print("Chi-square: %.3f (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)" % chi2)
print("Spot-check note: " + claimed_note)
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.")

Demo output

Entries (published, approximate): 5000
Spot-check sample: 25 entries across 4 categories (fixed seed 1143)
Category counts: {"chapters": 5, "headings": 5, "notes": 10, "subheadings": 5}
Expected per category: 6.25 entries
Chi-square: 3.000 (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)
Spot-check note: As published: the Harmonized System classification, more than 5,000 commodity groups in 99 chapters, current edition HS 2022, licensed from the WCO.
SHA-256 run 1: b8036223f8584091d1671b96da8b99452376a26f12640ffc45ad4a753877f6f6
SHA-256 run 2: b8036223f8584091d1671b96da8b99452376a26f12640ffc45ad4a753877f6f6
Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.
Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.

title = 'The WTO Tariff Download Facility'

categories = ['bound rates', 'applied rates', 'schedules', 'years']
trials = 25
seed = 1156
claimed_note = 'As published: bound and applied tariff downloads for WTO members, data from 1996 onward, free on the web.'

    counts = {c: 0 for c in categories}
    for _ in range(trials):
        counts[rng.choice(categories)] += 1
    return counts

expected = trials / len(categories)
counts_a = run_once()
counts_b = run_once()
chi2 = sum((o - expected) ** 2 / expected for o in counts_a.values())
result = {"title": title, "entries": entries, "trials": trials,
          "categories": len(categories), "seed": seed,
          "counts": counts_a, "chi_square": round(chi2, 3)}
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Catalog: " + title)
print("Entries (published, approximate): %d" % entries)
print("Spot-check sample: %d entries across %d categories (fixed seed %d)" % (trials, len(categories), seed))
print("Category counts: " + json.dumps(counts_a, sort_keys=True))
print("Expected per category: %.2f entries" % expected)
print("Chi-square: %.3f (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)" % chi2)
print("Spot-check note: " + claimed_note)
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.")

The Global Tariff Database Rank 14 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Design study: this record describes an original Signature design for The Global Tariff Database, a design study that presents no public fact as established and no design as a record.

The Global Tariff Database, built by Feodora Teti, provides bilateral statutory tariff rates for 200 importers and their partners, spanning 1988 to 2021, at the HS six-digit level. It fills the gaps in published tariff data with a documented algorithm.

The question behind The Global Tariff Database is a catalog question: what does this trade database hold, who keeps it, and how does a reader reach the public record inside it? The Global Tariff Database, built by Feodora Teti, provides bilateral statutory tariff rates for 200 importers and their partners, spanning 1988 to 2021, at the HS six-digit level. It fills the gaps in published tariff data with a documented algorithm. That is the whole of the question, and this record answers it in order: what the database is, where its public record lives, what license and access method govern it, and what the Signature edition designs beside it. The question matters because trade databases are the shelves of the public record: a reader who cannot find the shelf cannot check the fact, and a fact that cannot be checked is only a rumor with good manners. Cataloguing the catalogues is therefore ordinary library work, done one database at a time, with the access terms stated plainly and nothing invented where the publisher has published nothing. A reader who opens The Global Tariff Database is looking for bilateral statutory tariff rates: the entries, the records, and the classifications that make up the public record of bilateral statutory tariff rates. The database answers by holding those records in one place, in one classification, with one search, instead of leaving them scattered across a hundred publisher sites and a thousand documents. That is the service a trade database performs: it gathers, it classifies, it keeps the record searchable, and it lets a reader ask a question and get an answer that can be cited. The public record inside it is described in this entry in full: what information exists, where it lives, what license covers it, and how a reader reaches it, whether through a website, a download, or a published endpoint. Where no public endpoint is published, this record says so plainly, because an honest catalog never invents a door that is not there. The Signature version of The Global Tariff Database is a separate matter, and it is kept separate in this record. The public edition describes what exists; the Signature edition designs what could be built beside it: an original Signature design that teaches, presents, or re-organizes the same subject without copying the public database and without presenting a design as an established record. The two editions share a title and a subject, and they differ in exactly that way: one reports, the other designs. This record therefore serves two readers at once: the reader who wants the public facts about The Global Tariff Database, and the reader who wants to see what a Signature edition of the same subject would look like. Both readers get the same honesty: the public facts are stated as published, the design is labelled a design, and the ceiling line at the end of every record states the generator's limit in plain words.

History

The database was published in 2024 with the CESifo working paper 'Missing Tariffs' by Feodora Teti; a United States trade-war extension followed in 2025. The beta version v_beta1-2024-12 was released in December 2024, and the U.S. Trade War extension, covering January 2018 to August 2025, was released in October 2025. The history above is the published history: dates and owners as the database's own publications state them, with later developments carried in the public-record section below. A reader who wants the full chronology should read the database's own about page and its published documentation, which are named in the verification path. This record gives the shape of the story, not a day-by-day chronicle, and it says so where a date is approximate rather than exact. What matters for the catalog is not the day the database opened but the continuity: the record has been kept, the classification has been maintained, and the public can still reach it, which is why it belongs in this pair. The dates in this record are the dates the operator publishes; where the operator gives only a decade or an era, this record gives the decade or the era rather than inventing a year.

Public record

The Global Tariff Database, built by Feodora Teti, provides bilateral statutory tariff rates for 200 importers and their partners, spanning 1988 to 2021, at the HS six-digit level. It fills the gaps in published tariff data with a documented algorithm. It holds more than 6.9 billion observations of bilateral tariff rates, covering most-favoured-nation and preferential tariffs. Aggregated pair-level and sector-level data are published free on the author's site in .dta and .csv formats. The detailed HS6 microdata are not publicly available during the beta phase, which this record states plainly. No public API is published. That is the public record in outline. In detail, the record holds the subject in the form the operator publishes it: entries, classifications, and search tools, with the operator's own documentation as the guide to what each field means. The license and access terms above are the whole of the terms this record states, because they are the whole of the terms the publisher has published: where a term is unpublished, this record says so rather than guessing. A reader who needs more than the outline should follow the verification path, which names the database's own site and documentation; the catalog entry is a map to the record, and the record itself lives where the operator keeps it. The public record is also a living record: the operator adds entries, corrects entries, and revises classifications, so the counts in this entry are the counts as published at the time of writing, and a reader should check the live database for the current figure. Nothing in this record is a copy of the database itself: the catalog entry describes the database, and the database itself is reached through the links and the verification path.

Methods

Open the database's own site from the verification path and read its about page to confirm the operator and the coverage; Search the database for one bilateral statutory tariff rates entry you already know, and check that the entry you find matches what you expected; Read the license and access terms on the operator's own terms page, and note whether a public endpoint is published; View or download one sample of the data in the form the operator publishes it, and check its classification against this record's description; Run the demo program beside this record and compare its output with the stored output, to confirm the sampling statistics; Read the Signature design in the signature edition and note what it adds without copying the public database.

Signature version

The Signature edition designs a Global Tariff companion: a plain-language guide to one bilateral tariff pair, showing the most-favoured-nation rate, the preferential rate, and the gap between them, with a worked example of how a missing tariff is filled by the documented method. The design adds a timeline of one tariff pair across the 34 years the database covers. The design is original Signature work: it borrows the subject of the public database and nothing else, and it is labelled a design study in every place it appears, so that no reader mistakes it for an established public record. The design is judged as a design: whether it teaches the subject plainly, whether it presents the subject honestly, and whether a reader who follows it learns the subject rather than a copy of the database. The Signature edition keeps the same title and the same ladder rank as the public edition, so the two can be read side by side, and the difference between them is exactly the difference between reporting what exists and designing what could be built beside it.

Limits and cautions

The limits of this record are the limits of a catalog entry. It describes The Global Tariff Database; it does not contain it, and it cannot answer a question that only the database itself can answer, such as the current value of a single entry or the current text of a single record. The counts and dates in this record are snapshots from the operator's publications at the time of writing, and a living database will have moved on; the record says where the live figure can be checked, and a reader should check it. Where the operator publishes no public endpoint or no public license, this record says so, and the absence is a fact about the operator's publishing, not a gap in the catalog. The Signature design beside this record is a design study, not an established public record, and it must not be cited as evidence about the real database. Finally, this record is one of twenty-four in its pair, and it is written to be read beside the other twenty-three: no single entry carries the whole subject, and the field guide index record exists to hold the map.

  • Andres Rodriguez-Clare, Mauricio Ulate, Jose P. Vasquez, and Roman D. Zarate (co-authors of the trade-war extension)
  • CESifo (publisher of the working paper)

Dataset

It holds more than 6.9 billion observations of bilateral tariff rates, covering most-favoured-nation and preferential tariffs. The dataset behind this record is the published record of the database itself: the counts, the classifications, and the access terms stated above, taken from the operator's own publications and from the live database's own pages. It is not a dump of the database's contents, which would be a rebuild rather than a catalog entry, and this pair never rebuilds the databases it catalogues. The dataset is therefore small and honest: one entry per database, with the facts that let a reader find the database, understand what it holds, and reach it. The demo beside this record samples from a fixed seed to show the statistics of sampling; it does not reproduce the database's contents, and it says so in its own output. Where the operator publishes bulk downloads, this record notes them in the public-record section; where it does not, the record says so. The figures in this entry are the figures the operator publishes, and any figure that is an estimate is labelled as an estimate in the text. The counts in this entry are the counts as published at the time of writing, and a living database grows, so a reader should check the live database for the current figure.

Generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Science proof

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. The demo runs that audit twice with the same seed and hashes both results, so a fresh run verifies the arithmetic exactly. The audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic; it does not query the live database and it proves nothing about the live holdings, which must be checked against the published source itself. The science of the Global Tariff Database is the science of missing data: published tariff schedules have gaps, and the database fills them with a documented algorithm whose steps are published in the accompanying paper, so every filled value can be traced to its method. In plain words, the demo draws 25 entries at random with a fixed seed across 4 categories, so each category should receive about 6.25 entries by chance alone. The demo's simulated run gave counts {"MFN rates": 7, "pairs": 8, "preferential rates": 7, "sectors": 3} and a chi-square of 2.360. A chi-square near zero is exactly what a flat random draw produces; a large chi-square would need a reason, and the audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic, not that the live database is balanced.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic: expected per category E = 25 / 4 = 6.25. The demo's simulated counts were {"MFN rates": 7, "pairs": 8, "preferential rates": 7, "sectors": 3}. Chi-square = the sum over the 4 categories of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected, which comes to 2.360. The demo runs the sample twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

χ² = Σ (O_i − E)² / E, E = trials/categories (stated, not machine-checked)

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math
import random

title = 'The Global Tariff Database'
entries = 200
categories = ['MFN rates', 'preferential rates', 'pairs', 'sectors']
trials = 25
seed = 1169
claimed_note = 'As published: bilateral statutory tariff rates for 200 importers, 1988 to 2021, more than 6.9 billion observations; aggregated data free, microdata not public during the beta phase.'

def run_once():
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    counts = {c: 0 for c in categories}
    for _ in range(trials):
        counts[rng.choice(categories)] += 1
    return counts

expected = trials / len(categories)
counts_a = run_once()
counts_b = run_once()
chi2 = sum((o - expected) ** 2 / expected for o in counts_a.values())
result = {"title": title, "entries": entries, "trials": trials,
          "categories": len(categories), "seed": seed,
          "counts": counts_a, "chi_square": round(chi2, 3)}
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Catalog: " + title)
print("Entries (published, approximate): %d" % entries)
print("Spot-check sample: %d entries across %d categories (fixed seed %d)" % (trials, len(categories), seed))
print("Category counts: " + json.dumps(counts_a, sort_keys=True))
print("Expected per category: %.2f entries" % expected)
print("Chi-square: %.3f (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)" % chi2)
print("Spot-check note: " + claimed_note)
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.")

Entries (published, approximate): 200
Spot-check sample: 25 entries across 4 categories (fixed seed 1169)
Category counts: {"MFN rates": 7, "pairs": 8, "preferential rates": 7, "sectors": 3}
Expected per category: 6.25 entries
Chi-square: 2.360 (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)
Spot-check note: As published: bilateral statutory tariff rates for 200 importers, 1988 to 2021, more than 6.9 billion observations; aggregated data free, microdata not public during the beta phase.
SHA-256 run 1: 7ad6ecca86bc42d4c0a86dbc24022b9f7eada3edaea0611af814c77b7a20f164
SHA-256 run 2: 7ad6ecca86bc42d4c0a86dbc24022b9f7eada3edaea0611af814c77b7a20f164
Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.
Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.

χ² = Σ (O_i − E)² / E, E = trials/categories (stated, not machine-checked)

Scientific equation

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. The demo runs that audit twice with the same seed and hashes both results, so a fresh run verifies the arithmetic exactly. The audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic; it does not query the live database and it proves nothing about the live holdings, which must be checked against the published source itself. The science of the RTA database is the science of notification and transparency: an agreement exists as a legal text and as a notification, and the database must keep the text, the notification, and the status together or the record misleads. In plain words, the demo draws 25 entries at random with a fixed seed across 4 categories, so each category should receive about 6.25 entries by chance alone. The demo's simulated run gave counts {"early announcement": 3, "in force": 7, "signed": 8, "under negotiation": 7} and a chi-square of 2.360. A chi-square near zero is exactly what a flat random draw produces; a large chi-square would need a reason, and the audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic, not that the live database is balanced.

Proof sketch

Worked arithmetic: expected per category E = 25 / 4 = 6.25. The demo's simulated counts were {"early announcement": 3, "in force": 7, "signed": 8, "under negotiation": 7}. Chi-square = the sum over the 4 categories of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected, which comes to 2.360. The demo runs the sample twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Source

Signature trade database design, compiled November 2026

JAHDB/trade-databases-signature#JAH-TRDDS-000015 · k=cabe69e2

Evidence links

Spec: filed at merge; Wiki: JAH Wiki entry filed at merge; Dictionary: Dictionary definition filed at merge.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math
import random

title = 'The WTO Regional Trade Agreements Database'
entries = 350
categories = ['in force', 'signed', 'under negotiation', 'early announcement']
trials = 25
seed = 1182
claimed_note = 'As published: several hundred regional trade agreements, with texts, member lists, and status, free on the WTO website.'

def run_once():
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    counts = {c: 0 for c in categories}
    for _ in range(trials):
        counts[rng.choice(categories)] += 1
    return counts

expected = trials / len(categories)
counts_a = run_once()
counts_b = run_once()
chi2 = sum((o - expected) ** 2 / expected for o in counts_a.values())
result = {"title": title, "entries": entries, "trials": trials,
          "categories": len(categories), "seed": seed,
          "counts": counts_a, "chi_square": round(chi2, 3)}
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Catalog: " + title)
print("Entries (published, approximate): %d" % entries)
print("Spot-check sample: %d entries across %d categories (fixed seed %d)" % (trials, len(categories), seed))
print("Category counts: " + json.dumps(counts_a, sort_keys=True))
print("Expected per category: %.2f entries" % expected)
print("Chi-square: %.3f (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)" % chi2)
print("Spot-check note: " + claimed_note)
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.")

Demo output

Catalog: The WTO Regional Trade Agreements Database
Entries (published, approximate): 350
Spot-check sample: 25 entries across 4 categories (fixed seed 1182)
Category counts: {"early announcement": 3, "in force": 7, "signed": 8, "under negotiation": 7}
Expected per category: 6.25 entries
Chi-square: 2.360 (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)
Spot-check note: As published: several hundred regional trade agreements, with texts, member lists, and status, free on the WTO website.
SHA-256 run 1: 99c3dbb1f08dac5d19eab05e10f76447d07dbf5f6a940a016c089df5d5a29ef6
SHA-256 run 2: 99c3dbb1f08dac5d19eab05e10f76447d07dbf5f6a940a016c089df5d5a29ef6
Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.
Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.
Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.

Panjiva Trade Records Rank 16 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Design study: this record describes an original Signature design for Panjiva Trade Records, a design study that presents no public fact as established and no design as a record.

Panjiva, from S&P Global Market Intelligence, is a subscription database of global trade records built from bills of lading and customs filings, used to research suppliers and shipments.

The question behind Panjiva Trade Records is a catalog question: what does this trade database hold, who keeps it, and how does a reader reach the public record inside it? Panjiva, from S&P Global Market Intelligence, is a subscription database of global trade records built from bills of lading and customs filings, used to research suppliers and shipments. That is the whole of the question, and this record answers it in order: what the database is, where its public record lives, what license and access method govern it, and what the Signature edition designs beside it. The question matters because trade databases are the shelves of the public record: a reader who cannot find the shelf cannot check the fact, and a fact that cannot be checked is only a rumor with good manners. Cataloguing the catalogues is therefore ordinary library work, done one database at a time, with the access terms stated plainly and nothing invented where the publisher has published nothing. A reader who opens Panjiva Trade Records is looking for global shipment records: the entries, the records, and the classifications that make up the public record of global shipment records. The database answers by holding those records in one place, in one classification, with one search, instead of leaving them scattered across a hundred publisher sites and a thousand documents. That is the service a trade database performs: it gathers, it classifies, it keeps the record searchable, and it lets a reader ask a question and get an answer that can be cited. The public record inside it is described in this entry in full: what information exists, where it lives, what license covers it, and how a reader reaches it, whether through a website, a download, or a published endpoint. Where no public endpoint is published, this record says so plainly, because an honest catalog never invents a door that is not there. The Signature version of Panjiva Trade Records is a separate matter, and it is kept separate in this record. The public edition describes what exists; the Signature edition designs what could be built beside it: an original Signature design that teaches, presents, or re-organizes the same subject without copying the public database and without presenting a design as an established record. The two editions share a title and a subject, and they differ in exactly that way: one reports, the other designs. This record therefore serves two readers at once: the reader who wants the public facts about Panjiva Trade Records, and the reader who wants to see what a Signature edition of the same subject would look like. Both readers get the same honesty: the public facts are stated as published, the design is labelled a design, and the ceiling line at the end of every record states the generator's limit in plain words.

History

Panjiva was founded in 2006 and acquired by S&P Global in 2018; it built its record base from United States import filings and then expanded worldwide. Panjiva expanded from United States imports to global coverage and added supplier profiles, shipment tracking, and trade-lane analytics. The history above is the published history: dates and owners as the database's own publications state them, with later developments carried in the public-record section below. A reader who wants the full chronology should read the database's own about page and its published documentation, which are named in the verification path. This record gives the shape of the story, not a day-by-day chronicle, and it says so where a date is approximate rather than exact. What matters for the catalog is not the day the database opened but the continuity: the record has been kept, the classification has been maintained, and the public can still reach it, which is why it belongs in this pair. The dates in this record are the dates the operator publishes; where the operator gives only a decade or an era, this record gives the decade or the era rather than inventing a year.

Public record

Panjiva, from S&P Global Market Intelligence, is a subscription database of global trade records built from bills of lading and customs filings, used to research suppliers and shipments. It holds hundreds of millions of shipment records, covering imports and exports for many countries. Panjiva is a paid subscription service; a limited free search is offered on its website. No free public API is published for the full database. That is the public record in outline. In detail, the record holds the subject in the form the operator publishes it: entries, classifications, and search tools, with the operator's own documentation as the guide to what each field means. The license and access terms above are the whole of the terms this record states, because they are the whole of the terms the publisher has published: where a term is unpublished, this record says so rather than guessing. A reader who needs more than the outline should follow the verification path, which names the database's own site and documentation; the catalog entry is a map to the record, and the record itself lives where the operator keeps it. The public record is also a living record: the operator adds entries, corrects entries, and revises classifications, so the counts in this entry are the counts as published at the time of writing, and a reader should check the live database for the current figure. Nothing in this record is a copy of the database itself: the catalog entry describes the database, and the database itself is reached through the links and the verification path.

Signature version

The Signature edition designs a Panjiva companion: a supplier-research page for one company, showing its shipments, its suppliers, and its trade lanes on one page, each explained in plain words, with a worked example of checking one shipment against its bill of lading. The design adds a plain-language guide to reading a bill of lading. The design is original Signature work: it borrows the subject of the public database and nothing else, and it is labelled a design study in every place it appears, so that no reader mistakes it for an established public record. The design is judged as a design: whether it teaches the subject plainly, whether it presents the subject honestly, and whether a reader who follows it learns the subject rather than a copy of the database. The Signature edition keeps the same title and the same ladder rank as the public edition, so the two can be read side by side, and the difference between them is exactly the difference between reporting what exists and designing what could be built beside it.

The science of Panjiva is the science of shipment records: a bill of lading is a commercial document with a shipper, a consignee, a product, and a port, and the database's job is to make those four facts searchable across millions of filings. Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math
import random

title = 'Panjiva Trade Records'
entries = 100000000
categories = ['shipments', 'suppliers', 'consignees', 'lanes']
trials = 25
seed = 1195
claimed_note = 'As published: hundreds of millions of shipment records from bills of lading and customs filings, by paid subscription with a limited free search.'

def run_once():
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    counts = {c: 0 for c in categories}
    for _ in range(trials):
        counts[rng.choice(categories)] += 1
    return counts

expected = trials / len(categories)
counts_a = run_once()
counts_b = run_once()
chi2 = sum((o - expected) ** 2 / expected for o in counts_a.values())
result = {"title": title, "entries": entries, "trials": trials,
          "categories": len(categories), "seed": seed,
          "counts": counts_a, "chi_square": round(chi2, 3)}
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Catalog: " + title)
print("Entries (published, approximate): %d" % entries)
print("Spot-check sample: %d entries across %d categories (fixed seed %d)" % (trials, len(categories), seed))
print("Category counts: " + json.dumps(counts_a, sort_keys=True))
print("Expected per category: %.2f entries" % expected)
print("Chi-square: %.3f (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)" % chi2)
print("Spot-check note: " + claimed_note)
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.")

The published source for this record is importyeti.com.

Generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Science proof

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. The demo runs that audit twice with the same seed and hashes both results, so a fresh run verifies the arithmetic exactly. The audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic; it does not query the live database and it proves nothing about the live holdings, which must be checked against the published source itself. The science of the ImportYeti index is the science of search: millions of bills of lading are useful only if a reader can find one company in them, so the index must normalize company names that are spelled a dozen different ways. In plain words, the demo draws 25 entries at random with a fixed seed across 4 categories, so each category should receive about 6.25 entries by chance alone. The demo's simulated run gave counts {"companies": 7, "ports": 6, "products": 4, "suppliers": 8} and a chi-square of 1.400. A chi-square near zero is exactly what a flat random draw produces; a large chi-square would need a reason, and the audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic, not that the live database is balanced.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic: expected per category E = 25 / 4 = 6.25. The demo's simulated counts were {"companies": 7, "ports": 6, "products": 4, "suppliers": 8}. Chi-square = the sum over the 4 categories of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected, which comes to 1.400. The demo runs the sample twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

JAHDB/trade-databases-signature#JAH-TRDDS-000017 · k=f7d7204d

Evidence links

Spec: filed at merge; Wiki: JAH Wiki entry filed at merge; Dictionary: Dictionary definition filed at merge.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math
import random

title = 'ImportYeti Bill of Lading Index'
entries = 10000000
categories = ['companies', 'products', 'ports', 'suppliers']
trials = 25
seed = 1208
claimed_note = 'As published: millions of United States import bills of lading, free to search on the web.'

def run_once():
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    counts = {c: 0 for c in categories}
    for _ in range(trials):
        counts[rng.choice(categories)] += 1
    return counts

expected = trials / len(categories)
counts_a = run_once()
counts_b = run_once()
chi2 = sum((o - expected) ** 2 / expected for o in counts_a.values())
result = {"title": title, "entries": entries, "trials": trials,
          "categories": len(categories), "seed": seed,
          "counts": counts_a, "chi_square": round(chi2, 3)}
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Catalog: " + title)
print("Entries (published, approximate): %d" % entries)
print("Spot-check sample: %d entries across %d categories (fixed seed %d)" % (trials, len(categories), seed))
print("Category counts: " + json.dumps(counts_a, sort_keys=True))
print("Expected per category: %.2f entries" % expected)
print("Chi-square: %.3f (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)" % chi2)
print("Spot-check note: " + claimed_note)
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.")

Demo output

Entries (published, approximate): 10000000
Spot-check sample: 25 entries across 4 categories (fixed seed 1208)
Category counts: {"companies": 7, "ports": 6, "products": 4, "suppliers": 8}
Expected per category: 6.25 entries
Chi-square: 1.400 (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)
Spot-check note: As published: millions of United States import bills of lading, free to search on the web.
SHA-256 run 1: 06999097bfa76fce21db6b6549bc67c98bfee4f0605b0dc5d145107a6eb498f2
SHA-256 run 2: 06999097bfa76fce21db6b6549bc67c98bfee4f0605b0dc5d145107a6eb498f2
Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.
Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.

The Observatory of Economic Complexity Rank 18 · complex

Evidence: design-study Design study: this record describes an original Signature design for The Observatory of Economic Complexity, a design study that presents no public fact as established and no design as a record.

The Observatory of Economic Complexity (OEC) is a free data-visualization platform for world trade, built at the MIT Media Lab and now run by Datawheel, showing what countries export and import and how complex their economies are.

The question behind The Observatory of Economic Complexity is a catalog question: what does this trade database hold, who keeps it, and how does a reader reach the public record inside it? The Observatory of Economic Complexity (OEC) is a free data-visualization platform for world trade, built at the MIT Media Lab and now run by Datawheel, showing what countries export and import and how complex their economies are. That is the whole of the question, and this record answers it in order: what the database is, where its public record lives, what license and access method govern it, and what the Signature edition designs beside it. The question matters because trade databases are the shelves of the public record: a reader who cannot find the shelf cannot check the fact, and a fact that cannot be checked is only a rumor with good manners. Cataloguing the catalogues is therefore ordinary library work, done one database at a time, with the access terms stated plainly and nothing invented where the publisher has published nothing. A reader who opens The Observatory of Economic Complexity is looking for world trade visualizations and economic complexity: the entries, the records, and the classifications that make up the public record of world trade visualizations and economic complexity. The database answers by holding those records in one place, in one classification, with one search, instead of leaving them scattered across a hundred publisher sites and a thousand documents. That is the service a trade database performs: it gathers, it classifies, it keeps the record searchable, and it lets a reader ask a question and get an answer that can be cited. The public record inside it is described in this entry in full: what information exists, where it lives, what license covers it, and how a reader reaches it, whether through a website, a download, or a published endpoint. Where no public endpoint is published, this record says so plainly, because an honest catalog never invents a door that is not there. The Signature version of The Observatory of Economic Complexity is a separate matter, and it is kept separate in this record. The public edition describes what exists; the Signature edition designs what could be built beside it: an original Signature design that teaches, presents, or re-organizes the same subject without copying the public database and without presenting a design as an established record. The two editions share a title and a subject, and they differ in exactly that way: one reports, the other designs. This record therefore serves two readers at once: the reader who wants the public facts about The Observatory of Economic Complexity, and the reader who wants to see what a Signature edition of the same subject would look like. Public record

Dataset

It covers bilateral trade for more than 200 countries at the HS six-digit level, from 1994 onward, in versions updated as new Comtrade data arrive. The dataset behind this record is the published record of the database itself: the counts, the classifications, and the access terms stated above, taken from the operator's own publications and from the live database's own pages. Science proof

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. The demo runs that audit twice with the same seed and hashes both results, so a fresh run verifies the arithmetic exactly. The audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic; it does not query the live database and it proves nothing about the live holdings, which must be checked against the published source itself. The science of BACI is the science of reconciliation: the same shipment is reported twice with different values, and the database must choose one consistent value by a documented rule, so that every flow in the database can be compared with every other. In plain words, the demo draws 25 entries at random with a fixed seed across 4 categories, so each category should receive about 6.25 entries by chance alone. The demo's simulated run gave counts {"flows": 14, "partners": 4, "products": 2, "years": 5} and a chi-square of 13.560. A chi-square near zero is exactly what a flat random draw produces; a large chi-square would need a reason, and the audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic, not that the live database is balanced.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic: expected per category E = 25 / 4 = 6.25. The demo's simulated counts were {"flows": 14, "partners": 4, "products": 2, "years": 5}. Chi-square = the sum over the 4 categories of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected, which comes to 13.560. The demo runs the sample twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

χ² = Σ (O_i − E)² / E, E = trials/categories (stated, not machine-checked)

Scientific equation

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. The demo runs that audit twice with the same seed and hashes both results, so a fresh run verifies the arithmetic exactly. Worked arithmetic: expected per category E = 25 / 4 = 6.25. The demo's simulated counts were {"flows": 14, "partners": 4, "products": 2, "years": 5}. Signature trade database design, compiled November 2026
