Evidence links

Spec: filed at merge; Wiki: JAH Wiki entry filed at merge; Dictionary: Dictionary definition filed at merge.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math
import random

title = 'The BACI Trade Database (CEPII)'
entries = 200
categories = ['flows', 'partners', 'products', 'years']
trials = 25
seed = 1234
claimed_note = 'As published: reconciled bilateral trade for more than 200 countries at HS six-digit level, from 1994 onward, free to download from CEPII.'

def run_once():
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    counts = {c: 0 for c in categories}
    for _ in range(trials):
        counts[rng.choice(categories)] += 1
    return counts

expected = trials / len(categories)
counts_a = run_once()
counts_b = run_once()
chi2 = sum((o - expected) ** 2 / expected for o in counts_a.values())
result = {"title": title, "entries": entries, "trials": trials,
          "categories": len(categories), "seed": seed,
          "counts": counts_a, "chi_square": round(chi2, 3)}
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Catalog: " + title)
print("Entries (published, approximate): %d" % entries)
print("Spot-check sample: %d entries across %d categories (fixed seed %d)" % (trials, len(categories), seed))
print("Category counts: " + json.dumps(counts_a, sort_keys=True))
print("Expected per category: %.2f entries" % expected)
print("Chi-square: %.3f (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)" % chi2)
print("Spot-check note: " + claimed_note)
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.")

Demo output

Catalog: The BACI Trade Database (CEPII)
Entries (published, approximate): 200
Spot-check sample: 25 entries across 4 categories (fixed seed 1234)
Category counts: {"flows": 14, "partners": 4, "products": 2, "years": 5}
Expected per category: 6.25 entries
Chi-square: 13.560 (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)
Spot-check note: As published: reconciled bilateral trade for more than 200 countries at HS six-digit level, from 1994 onward, free to download from CEPII.
SHA-256 run 1: 6a3746ada0b08235f7eb927e467fad0541645f222edd1825b422ca479268c87a
SHA-256 run 2: 6a3746ada0b08235f7eb927e467fad0541645f222edd1825b422ca479268c87a
Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.
Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.
Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.

The GTAP Database Rank 20 · complex

Evidence: design-study Design study: this record describes an original Signature design for The GTAP Database, a design study that presents no public fact as established and no design as a record.

The GTAP Database, from Purdue University's Global Trade Analysis Project, is a global database of production, trade, and protection data used to run computable general equilibrium models of the world economy.

The question behind The GTAP Database is a catalog question: what does this trade database hold, who keeps it, and how does a reader reach the public record inside it? The GTAP Database, from Purdue University's Global Trade Analysis Project, is a global database of production, trade, and protection data used to run computable general equilibrium models of the world economy. That is the whole of the question, and this record answers it in order: what the database is, where its public record lives, what license and access method govern it, and what the Signature edition designs beside it. The question matters because trade databases are the shelves of the public record: a reader who cannot find the shelf cannot check the fact, and a fact that cannot be checked is only a rumor with good manners. Cataloguing the catalogues is therefore ordinary library work, done one database at a time, with the access terms stated plainly and nothing invented where the publisher has published nothing. A reader who opens The GTAP Database is looking for global production, trade, and protection data: the entries, the records, and the classifications that make up the public record of global production, trade, and protection data. The database answers by holding those records in one place, in one classification, with one search, instead of leaving them scattered across a hundred publisher sites and a thousand documents. That is the service a trade database performs: it gathers, it classifies, it keeps the record searchable, and it lets a reader ask a question and get an answer that can be cited. The public record inside it is described in this entry in full: what information exists, where it lives, what license covers it, and how a reader reaches it, whether through a website, a download, or a published endpoint. Where no public endpoint is published, this record says so plainly, because an honest catalog never invents a door that is not there. The Signature version of The GTAP Database is a separate matter, and it is kept separate in this record. The public edition describes what exists; the Signature edition designs what could be built beside it: an original Signature design that teaches, presents, or re-organizes the same subject without copying the public database and without presenting a design as an established record. The two editions share a title and a subject, and they differ in exactly that way: one reports, the other designs. This record therefore serves two readers at once: the reader who wants the public facts about The GTAP Database, and the reader who wants to see what a Signature edition of the same subject would look like. Both readers get the same honesty: the public facts are stated as published, the design is labelled a design, and the ceiling line at the end of every record states the generator's limit in plain words.

History

The Global Trade Analysis Project was founded at Purdue University in 1992, and the database is released in numbered versions, most recently GTAP 11. GTAP releases expanded from a small set of regions to worldwide coverage, and the project added energy, land-use, and emissions satellite data. The history above is the published history: dates and owners as the database's own publications state them, with later developments carried in the public-record section below. A reader who wants the full chronology should read the database's own about page and its published documentation, which are named in the verification path. This record gives the shape of the story, not a day-by-day chronicle, and it says so where a date is approximate rather than exact. What matters for the catalog is not the day the database opened but the continuity: the record has been kept, the classification has been maintained, and the public can still reach it, which is why it belongs in this pair. The dates in this record are the dates the operator publishes; where the operator gives only a decade or an era, this record gives the decade or the era rather than inventing a year.

Public record

The GTAP Database, from Purdue University's Global Trade Analysis Project, is a global database of production, trade, and protection data used to run computable general equilibrium models of the world economy. GTAP 11 covers 160 regions and 65 sectors, with bilateral trade, tariffs, and input-output data. The GTAP Database is licensed by Purdue University; a free aggregated version and a public data portal are offered, while the full database requires a license. No free public API is published for the full database. That is the public record in outline. In detail, the record holds the subject in the form the operator publishes it: entries, classifications, and search tools, with the operator's own documentation as the guide to what each field means. The license and access terms above are the whole of the terms this record states, because they are the whole of the terms the publisher has published: where a term is unpublished, this record says so rather than guessing. A reader who needs more than the outline should follow the verification path, which names the database's own site and documentation; the catalog entry is a map to the record, and the record itself lives where the operator keeps it. The public record is also a living record: the operator adds entries, corrects entries, and revises classifications, so the counts in this entry are the counts as published at the time of writing, and a reader should check the live database for the current figure. Nothing in this record is a copy of the database itself: the catalog entry describes the database, and the database itself is reached through the links and the verification path.

Methods

Open the database's own site from the verification path and read its about page to confirm the operator and the coverage; Search the database for one global production, trade, and protection data entry you already know, and check that the entry you find matches what you expected; Read the license and access terms on the operator's own terms page, and note whether a public endpoint is published; View or download one sample of the data in the form the operator publishes it, and check its classification against this record's description; Run the demo program beside this record and compare its output with the stored output, to confirm the sampling statistics; Read the Signature design in the signature edition and note what it adds without copying the public database.

Signature version

The Signature edition designs a GTAP companion: a plain-language page for one region's economy, showing its production, its trade, and its protection on one page, with a worked example of reading one sector's input-output row. The design adds a teaching page that explains what a general equilibrium model does with the data. The design is original Signature work: it borrows the subject of the public database and nothing else, and it is labelled a design study in every place it appears, so that no reader mistakes it for an established public record. The design is judged as a design: whether it teaches the subject plainly, whether it presents the subject honestly, and whether a reader who follows it learns the subject rather than a copy of the database. The Signature edition keeps the same title and the same ladder rank as the public edition, so the two can be read side by side, and the difference between them is exactly the difference between reporting what exists and designing what could be built beside it.

Public record

The Eora Global Supply Chain Database is a multi-region input-output database that traces goods and services through global supply chains, built by the University of Sydney's Integrated Sustainability Analysis group. Eora26 covers 189 countries and 26 sectors, with time series from 1990 onward, used for carbon, water, and labour footprint studies. Eora is free for research use with registration; commercial use is licensed. No public API is published. That is the public record in outline. A reader who needs more than the outline should follow the verification path, which names the database's own site and documentation; the catalog entry is a map to the record, and the record itself lives where the operator keeps it. The public record is also a living record: the operator adds entries, corrects entries, and revises classifications, so the counts in this entry are the counts as published at the time of writing, and a reader should check the live database for the current figure. Nothing in this record is a copy of the database itself: the catalog entry describes the database, and the database itself is reached through the links and the verification path.

Signature version

The Signature edition designs an Eora companion: a supply-chain story for one product, tracing its inputs back through the countries and sectors that made them, with the carbon footprint of each step shown in plain words. The design adds a worked example of reading one footprint from the table to the total. The design is original Signature work: it borrows the subject of the public database and nothing else, and it is labelled a design study in every place it appears, so that no reader mistakes it for an established public record. The design is judged as a design: whether it teaches the subject plainly, whether it presents the subject honestly, and whether a reader who follows it learns the subject rather than a copy of the database. The Signature edition keeps the same title and the same ladder rank as the public edition, so the two can be read side by side, and the difference between them is exactly the difference between reporting what exists and designing what could be built beside it.

Science proof

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. The demo runs that audit twice with the same seed and hashes both results, so a fresh run verifies the arithmetic exactly. The audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic; it does not query the live database and it proves nothing about the live holdings, which must be checked against the published source itself. The science of Eora is the science of footprints: a product's carbon footprint is the sum of the emissions at every step of its supply chain, and the multi-region table is what lets those steps be added up across borders. In plain words, the demo draws 25 entries at random with a fixed seed across 5 categories, so each category should receive about 5.00 entries by chance alone. The demo's simulated run gave counts {"countries": 5, "emissions": 6, "labour": 2, "sectors": 6, "water": 6} and a chi-square of 2.400. A chi-square near zero is exactly what a flat random draw produces; a large chi-square would need a reason, and the audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic, not that the live database is balanced.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic: expected per category E = 25 / 5 = 5.00. The demo's simulated counts were {"countries": 5, "emissions": 6, "labour": 2, "sectors": 6, "water": 6}. Chi-square = the sum over the 5 categories of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected, which comes to 2.400. The demo runs the sample twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

χ² = Σ (O_i − E)² / E, E = trials/categories (stated, not machine-checked)

Scientific equation

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. Signature trade database design, compiled November 2026

JAHDB/trade-databases-signature#JAH-TRDDS-000021 · k=8638d19a

Evidence links

Spec: filed at merge; Wiki: JAH Wiki entry filed at merge; Dictionary: Dictionary definition filed at merge.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math
import random

title = 'The Eora Global Supply Chain Database'
entries = 189
categories = ['countries', 'sectors', 'emissions', 'water', 'labour']
trials = 25
seed = 1260
claimed_note = 'As published: Eora26 covers 189 countries and 26 sectors, from 1990 onward, free for research use with registration.'

def run_once():
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    counts = {c: 0 for c in categories}
    for _ in range(trials):
        counts[rng.choice(categories)] += 1
    return counts

expected = trials / len(categories)
counts_a = run_once()
counts_b = run_once()
chi2 = sum((o - expected) ** 2 / expected for o in counts_a.values())
result = {"title": title, "entries": entries, "trials": trials,
          "categories": len(categories), "seed": seed,
          "counts": counts_a, "chi_square": round(chi2, 3)}
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Catalog: " + title)
print("Entries (published, approximate): %d" % entries)
print("Spot-check sample: %d entries across %d categories (fixed seed %d)" % (trials, len(categories), seed))
print("Category counts: " + json.dumps(counts_a, sort_keys=True))
print("Expected per category: %.2f entries" % expected)
print("Chi-square: %.3f (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)" % chi2)
print("Spot-check note: " + claimed_note)
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.")

Demo output

Catalog: The Eora Global Supply Chain Database
Entries (published, approximate): 189
Spot-check sample: 25 entries across 5 categories (fixed seed 1260)
Category counts: {"countries": 5, "emissions": 6, "labour": 2, "sectors": 6, "water": 6}
Expected per category: 5.00 entries
Chi-square: 2.400 (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)
Spot-check note: As published: Eora26 covers 189 countries and 26 sectors, from 1990 onward, free for research use with registration.
SHA-256 run 1: 88e4580cd218aa846d78ce7a38be8a6b11b2153d30136a0674c0eb5bca344fc8
SHA-256 run 2: 88e4580cd218aa846d78ce7a38be8a6b11b2153d30136a0674c0eb5bca344fc8
Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.
Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.
Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.

The EXIOBASE Database Rank 22 · complex

Evidence: design-study Design study: this record describes an original Signature design for The EXIOBASE Database, a design study that presents no public fact as established and no design as a record.

EXIOBASE is a global multi-regional input-output database built by a European research consortium, used to trace the environmental and economic footprints of consumption.

The question behind The EXIOBASE Database is a catalog question: what does this trade database hold, who keeps it, and how does a reader reach the public record inside it? EXIOBASE is a global multi-regional input-output database built by a European research consortium, used to trace the environmental and economic footprints of consumption. That is the whole of the question, and this record answers it in order: what the database is, where its public record lives, what license and access method govern it, and what the Signature edition designs beside it. The question matters because trade databases are the shelves of the public record: a reader who cannot find the shelf cannot check the fact, and a fact that cannot be checked is only a rumor with good manners. Cataloguing the catalogues is therefore ordinary library work, done one database at a time, with the access terms stated plainly and nothing invented where the publisher has published nothing. A reader who opens The EXIOBASE Database is looking for global multi-regional input-output footprints: the entries, the records, and the classifications that make up the public record of global multi-regional input-output footprints. The database answers by holding those records in one place, in one classification, with one search, instead of leaving them scattered across a hundred publisher sites and a thousand documents. That is the service a trade database performs: it gathers, it classifies, it keeps the record searchable, and it lets a reader ask a question and get an answer that can be cited. The public record inside it is described in this entry in full: what information exists, where it lives, what license covers it, and how a reader reaches it, whether through a website, a download, or a published endpoint. Where no public endpoint is published, this record says so plainly, because an honest catalog never invents a door that is not there. The Signature version of The EXIOBASE Database is a separate matter, and it is kept separate in this record. The public edition describes what exists; the Signature edition designs what could be built beside it: an original Signature design that teaches, presents, or re-organizes the same subject without copying the public database and without presenting a design as an established record. The two editions share a title and a subject, and they differ in exactly that way: one reports, the other designs. This record therefore serves two readers at once: the reader who wants the public facts about The EXIOBASE Database, and the reader who wants to see what a Signature edition of the same subject would look like. Both readers get the same honesty: the public facts are stated as published, the design is labelled a design, and the ceiling line at the end of every record states the generator's limit in plain words.

History

EXIOBASE was built under European research projects in the 2000s and 2010s; version 3 is the current release, maintained by the EXIOBASE consortium. Version 3 expanded product detail and added a monetary and hybrid supply-use version, and the database became a standard source for consumption-based footprint accounts. The history above is the published history: dates and owners as the database's own publications state them, with later developments carried in the public-record section below. A reader who wants the full chronology should read the database's own about page and its published documentation, which are named in the verification path. This record gives the shape of the story, not a day-by-day chronicle, and it says so where a date is approximate rather than exact. What matters for the catalog is not the day the database opened but the continuity: the record has been kept, the classification has been maintained, and the public can still reach it, which is why it belongs in this pair. The dates in this record are the dates the operator publishes; where the operator gives only a decade or an era, this record gives the decade or the era rather than inventing a year.

This record therefore serves two readers at once: the reader who wants the public facts about Trade Data Monitor, and the reader who wants to see what a Signature edition of the same subject would look like. History

Trade Data Monitor was founded in Geneva and has published trade statistics since the 2000s. The service added mirror-statistics tools that flag gaps between what an exporter reports and what the importer reports, and its data feed the WTO Analytical Database. The history above is the published history: dates and owners as the database's own publications state them, with later developments carried in the public-record section below. A reader who wants the full chronology should read the database's own about page and its published documentation, which are named in the verification path. This record gives the shape of the story, not a day-by-day chronicle, and it says so where a date is approximate rather than exact. What matters for the catalog is not the day the database opened but the continuity: the record has been kept, the classification has been maintained, and the public can still reach it, which is why it belongs in this pair. The dates in this record are the dates the operator publishes; where the operator gives only a decade or an era, this record gives the decade or the era rather than inventing a year.

Public record

Trade Data Monitor is a subscription service that publishes official trade statistics from national customs authorities, with tools for comparing import and export data across countries. It covers official trade data for more than 100 reporting countries, with monthly and annual flows. Trade Data Monitor is a paid subscription service; no free public API is published for the full database. That is the public record in outline. In detail, the record holds the subject in the form the operator publishes it: entries, classifications, and search tools, with the operator's own documentation as the guide to what each field means. The license and access terms above are the whole of the terms this record states, because they are the whole of the terms the publisher has published: where a term is unpublished, this record says so rather than guessing. The public record is also a living record: the operator adds entries, corrects entries, and revises classifications, so the counts in this entry are the counts as published at the time of writing, and a reader should check the live database for the current figure. Nothing in this record is a copy of the database itself: the catalog entry describes the database, and the database itself is reached through the links and the verification path.

Science proof

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. The demo runs that audit twice with the same seed and hashes both results, so a fresh run verifies the arithmetic exactly. The audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic; it does not query the live database and it proves nothing about the live holdings, which must be checked against the published source itself. The science of Trade Data Monitor is the science of mirror statistics: the same flow reported by two countries should match, and the gap between the two reports is itself information about valuation, timing, and classification. In plain words, the demo draws 25 entries at random with a fixed seed across 4 categories, so each category should receive about 6.25 entries by chance alone. The demo's simulated run gave counts {"exports": 6, "imports": 7, "mirror gaps": 8, "partners": 4} and a chi-square of 1.400. A chi-square near zero is exactly what a flat random draw produces; a large chi-square would need a reason, and the audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic, not that the live database is balanced.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic: expected per category E = 25 / 4 = 6.25. The demo's simulated counts were {"exports": 6, "imports": 7, "mirror gaps": 8, "partners": 4}. Chi-square = the sum over the 4 categories of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected, which comes to 1.400. The demo runs the sample twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

χ² = Σ (O_i − E)² / E, E = trials/categories (stated, not machine-checked)

Scientific equation

A catalog spot-check works like a quality audit: a fixed-seed sample of entries is drawn from the catalog's categories, the counts per category are compared with the flat expectation, and a chi-square figure measures how far the sample strays from flat. The demo runs that audit twice with the same seed and hashes both results, so a fresh run verifies the arithmetic exactly. The audit proves only that the demo's own arithmetic is deterministic; it does not query the live database and it proves nothing about the live holdings, which must be checked against the published source itself. Spec: filed at merge; Wiki: JAH Wiki entry filed at merge; Dictionary: Dictionary definition filed at merge.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math
import random

title = 'Trade Data Monitor'
entries = 100
categories = ['imports', 'exports', 'mirror gaps', 'partners']
trials = 25
seed = 1286
claimed_note = 'As published: official trade data for more than 100 reporting countries, by paid subscription; data feed the WTO Analytical Database.'

def run_once():
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    counts = {c: 0 for c in categories}
    for _ in range(trials):
        counts[rng.choice(categories)] += 1
    return counts

expected = trials / len(categories)
counts_a = run_once()
counts_b = run_once()
chi2 = sum((o - expected) ** 2 / expected for o in counts_a.values())
result = {"title": title, "entries": entries, "trials": trials,
          "categories": len(categories), "seed": seed,
          "counts": counts_a, "chi_square": round(chi2, 3)}
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Catalog: " + title)
print("Entries (published, approximate): %d" % entries)
print("Spot-check sample: %d entries across %d categories (fixed seed %d)" % (trials, len(categories), seed))
print("Category counts: " + json.dumps(counts_a, sort_keys=True))
print("Expected per category: %.2f entries" % expected)
print("Chi-square: %.3f (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)" % chi2)
print("Spot-check note: " + claimed_note)
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.")

Demo output

Catalog: Trade Data Monitor
Entries (published, approximate): 100
Spot-check sample: 25 entries across 4 categories (fixed seed 1286)
Category counts: {"exports": 6, "imports": 7, "mirror gaps": 8, "partners": 4}
Expected per category: 6.25 entries
Chi-square: 1.400 (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)
Spot-check note: As published: official trade data for more than 100 reporting countries, by paid subscription; data feed the WTO Analytical Database.
SHA-256 run 1: 8232b0d9a367f72c64451df995873b94f3489ddf9359f9f5d81cf70370bd4170
SHA-256 run 2: 8232b0d9a367f72c64451df995873b94f3489ddf9359f9f5d81cf70370bd4170
Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.
Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math
import random

title = 'The Trade Databases Field Guide Index'
entries = 24
categories = ['flow databases', 'tariff databases', 'model databases', 'guides']
trials = 25
seed = 1299
claimed_note = 'As published in this pair: a complete list of the twenty-three trade databases in this pair, with the question each one answers.'

def run_once():
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    counts = {c: 0 for c in categories}
    for _ in range(trials):
        counts[rng.choice(categories)] += 1
    return counts

expected = trials / len(categories)
counts_a = run_once()
counts_b = run_once()
chi2 = sum((o - expected) ** 2 / expected for o in counts_a.values())

          "categories": len(categories), "seed": seed,
          "counts": counts_a, "chi_square": round(chi2, 3)}
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Catalog: " + title)
print("Entries (published, approximate): %d" % entries)
print("Spot-check sample: %d entries across %d categories (fixed seed %d)" % (trials, len(categories), seed))
print("Category counts: " + json.dumps(counts_a, sort_keys=True))
print("Expected per category: %.2f entries" % expected)
print("Chi-square: %.3f (sum of (observed minus expected) squared, divided by expected)" % chi2)
print("Spot-check note: " + claimed_note)
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same sampled counts.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo simulates a seeded spot-check sample only. It does not query the live database, and a balanced sample is chance, not a verification of the live holdings.")
print("Design study: this record describes a Signature database design, not an established public record.")
